Moreover when silk, satin, or The procession was re-formed and returned to the brocade has done its duty in frock form, it can be Town Hall where light refreshments were served. drafted on to the underdress stage, and after that end its days as a petticoat. Arms and Sleeves.—Considering that a woman's fore- arm is—generally speaking—her weak point and that the upper part of her arm is very frequently a thing of beauty, it is distinctly unfortunate that Dame Fashion should have permitted us to see so much of the former, and so little of the latter during several seasons past. Happily we have at length been persuaded to cover our forearm in the daytime, and for evening wear the short puff sleeve is in vogue again, so there is a general improvement all round. The Paris dressmakers judging by the models just sent over, are dispensing with sleeves altogether when creating evening gowns, their place being taken by shoulder straps of jewelled lade and lengths of transparent materials which hang down wing-fashion. An Old-World Trimming.—Swansdown is greatly to the fore again, a state of affairs probably due to the increasing craze for white. At leading milliners many of the newest hats show crowns of swansdown while this fascinating and becoming trimming appears upon a number of the latest evening coats and cloaks. For the debutantes evening dress again, swansdown is in greet request, and mingled delightfully with foamy frills of lace or chiffon, trails of snowdrops or field daisies, or seed-peal embroideries. To chil,'ren swansdown is an ideal trimming and it is therefore a matter of congratu- lation that so much of it, should be in evidence in the juvenile departments. Grotesque Hat Trimmings.—The farmyard and the sportsmen's guns are providing for the making up of the latest huge mounts that wave and flutter in the breeze on the fashionable hat. They therefore need not offend the humanitarian, however much they may lean to the grotesque in their unwieldy appearance and behaviour in rough weather. Strange to say the small birds of tropical plumage have quite gone out of favour, but this perhaps is due not so much to questions of principle as to the craze to have everything on as large a scale as possible in the millinery world. Our National Cookery.—The decline of domesticity so commonly supposed to be a modern failure, but this view does not find support in the preface which M. Buckmaster has contributed to the catalogue of the Exhibition of the Universal Cookery and Food Associa- tion at the Horticultural Hall in London. Once, he says, English cookery was the best in Europe, but that after a series of ups and downs, it deg,enerated in the reigns of George 11. and George 111. until at last it was little better than that of the ancient Britons. Yet at that period, maids and housewives were supposed to take little interest in matters beyond the threshold of

of the newest and most modish type,, and is suit. able as an " odd" skirt for wear with flannel or silk shirts, or as part of a costume which com- prises a bolero or hip-long coat. This skirt is made with a succession of wide pleats all round, starting with a box-pleat in front, and finishing at the back with an inverted pleat, all the pleats being firmly machined to a point a little above the knees and then released. Serge, tweed, homespun, cloth, and frieze are all suitable materials for the realisation of this design.

BUYING A HAT.

II pity the uninitiated woman of quiet tastes who suddenly finds herself buying a hat in a ; large establishment at the present moment, says a writer in T. P.'s Weekly. She will be shown monstrosity after monstrosity. If her taste ob- jects to a high crown, with wide, drooping brim, trimmed with half-a-dozen enormous choux of ribbon of different colours, she will be shown a velvet hat, with a tani-o'-shanter crown resembling a hassock, and a couple of enormous feathers flying at ease from a point in the side. Does her bewildered face plainly say "No" to these temptations, a satin or silk Buffalo Bill shape, with a bunch of chrysanthemums, larger in size and more glaring in colour than was over attempted by nature, will be recommended as "sweetly pretty," or perhaps it is suggested that her taste might lie between it and a chenille "cavalier" overladen with a bow measuring more than half a yard from point to point. But let the buyer take heart of grace, let her be firm in her stand against exaggeration, and she will have her reward in seeing new models eventually brought forward, on which her quiet taste may dwell with approval. TO-DAY'S RECIPE. BRAISED TONGUE,--All unsalted ox tongue cooked thus is very delicious. Put the tongue in a saucepan of boiling water and simmer two hours. Remove tongue from water, skim it care- fully, and trim the roots. Place in a deep pan or earthen dish and surround with ilb. of salt pork cut in dice, a little carrot and celery cut in dice,, one onion cut the same, a spray of parsley, a bay leaf, a quarter teaspoonful pepper- corns, two cloves pour over two cupfuls brown stock, cover closely and bake in the oven one and a-half hour; turn once after cooking one hour. Let cool in the liquor in which it is oraisea; out before quite cola put it on a noara with a skewer through the roots and front part, so that when quite cold it may stand nicely on a dish. It should be glazed while on the board. and when dished must be garnished with parsley.

Use of the Kniie.

Fred Sitnpsom, Dakin's-yard, was charged at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday with an aggravated assault upon Bridget Cunliffe. The Chief Constable stated that the prosecutrix and the prisoner had cohabited together for twelve years. On Sunday last the prisoner went home to his dinner, when a quarrel arose, through him eating his dinner from a roasting tin. Lie struck her on the hand with a knife, causing a severe wound, which necessitated seven stitches. The Chairman (Mr. J. Rymer Young) said the use of the knife must be discouraged and stopped. A fine of 20s. and costs would be imposed.

At Kibworth, near Leicester, a turnip has been grown which turned the scale at 21i1b. It measured 33!, inches in circumference.

BT MARION WYMBORNE,

Mr. Faversham was never tired of dilating :n the beauties of the little gold god which he :)ad bought at an auction 'sale for a good •!ound sum. It was generally kept under lock- ind-key in the safe in Mr. Faversham's library, but sometimes after dinner he would bring it out to hand round to admiring guests. The e 5 es of the god were rubies and worth a small fortune, while the delicacy and i It- tricacy of the Indian workmanship were something to marvel at. In fact, it had but one serious rival in his affections, and that was his only child and heiress Cecilia, cetat twenty. Cecilia was charming to behold— dusky of hair, blue of eye, with a warm- tinted sun-kissed complexion. She was still disengaged—which was her fault, for at least half-a-dozen men were in love with Cecilia— which was not her fault. Of the half-dozen her father looked with most approval upon ins nephew, Philip Clements, who was practically an adopted son, and would someday, it was rumoured, be given a partnership in the big brewery firm of Faversham and Co. In every way the match would be most suitable. Philip simply adored his little cousin; but Cecilia possessed a will of her own, and thought him very slow, and uninteresting. There was nothing romantic about Philip, and Cecilia thirsted for a romantic wooing. "You see, Philip, old boy, it couldn't pos- sibly be a case of true love because it would run so smoothly." she told him. "Not even a ripple to disturb the even tenor of its way."

Philip smiled down at her with grave kind eyes.

"I don't think there would be much mono- toiiy where you were concerned." Cecilia laughed merrily. "Yes, I flatte, myself lam rather unexpected. But you— oh! Philip, I should always know exactly what you would do under any circumstances, and that leaves no scope for the imagina- tion."

"What should, I do?" he asked, amusedly. "Your duty, and that is so copy-boosh ; there is so much in life more interesting than doing one's duty."

It was soon after this that Cecilia met Adrian Graham, and it was a case of love at first sight. Adrian made a variable income by painting, and had come down on a three months' visit to his sister, Colonel Maclean's wife, ostensibly -IF—sketch llits of Blairton scenery. Possibly the hints his sister had thrown out as to the charms, personal and pecuniary, of Cecilia Faversham, might have had something to do with his eagerness to bury himself at Blairton. Mr. Faversham, unfortunately, did not "cotton," as he put it, to his would-be son- in-law. "A chap without an ounce of busi- ness ability in his composition," he said, con- temptuously. "No, Cecilia, my dear, I don't intend letting you throw yourself away on any painter-pauper" (Mr. Faversham became alliterative in his indignation) "however handsome he is."

Cecilia was indignant. "He has genius," she declared fiercely, "and I have money enough for both of us." "Did he tell you so? I fancy the money's mine, Cecilia, and you won't get a penny- piece of it unless you marry to please me." Cecilia cried. She didn't often, which made her tears the more effective now.

Mr. Faversham didn't like enacting the "stern parent," and at last relented suffi- ciently to say that if Adrian showed he had grit in him and at the end of twelve months had earned some four or five hundred pounds, Mr. Faversham would be prepared to give him a share in the brewery. Cecilia felt grateful for the concession, though the terms seemed prohibitive. Adrian had never sold a picture for more than fifty pounds, and orders did not come tumbling over each other's heels to get to him. Besides, he could only paint when he was inspired, he said. and inspirations are not articles of everyday consumption. However, when Mr. Faver- sham, in a personal interview, repeated his terms, Adrian declared his intention of ful- filling them Or perishing in the attempt. He was returning to town on the morrow, so he and Cecilia took a tender farewell amongst the palms in the conservatory. "Henceforth, sweetheart, my life will be one of stern, rigid self-denial," said Adrian. "No Carlton dinners, no theatres, no smokes. Ye" may picture me slaving away in my studio, thinking, always thinking, of you." Cecilia thrilled at the picture. How splen- did it was of him! Philip would never say and do such things for her. Carlton dinners were not much in his line, and as for tobacco she didn't believe he could exist without his horrid old pipe, whereas Adrian smoked only cigars. "It is wonderful how small econo- mies mount up into big savings ! " said Cecilia, wisely. "1 once started a money- box where you put sixpence in and can't get it out till you'ire saved twenty. I got ten shil- lings quite soon and bought a new collar for Snip-Snap." Snip-Snap was a particularly hideous bull-dog, whose beauty, as Cecilia pointed out, consisted in its ugliness. The money-box idea did not much appeal to Adrian, but he announced his intention of forswearing hansoms and adopting tubes and 'buses instead..

"You won't try too hard to save, will you ? " pleaded Cecilia. "I mean going without proper food and things?" Adrian would not promise. Undoubtedly he meant to carry out all his resolutions of economy. but— It was quite by chance that a few weeks later Mr. Faversham dified at the Carlton and saw him there. After- wards Adrian explained in a letter to Cecilia that he found to get about amongst people was good from a professional point of vie. "If they don't see you they forget about you, and an artist forgotten is better dead." One day Mr. Faversham realised that he did not feel BQ young as. ho used tu be—that the grasshopper, otherwise the big brewery, had become a burden. The doctor spoke of overwork, nervous dyspepsia, and advocated a complete change; so, leaving Philip to look after the brewery, Mr. Faversham started for a three months' holiday in Switzerland. „ .

"I've half a mind tO take the little god with me," he remarked to Cecilia the night before he started, as she sat perched on the arm of his chair in the library. "Oh, you superstitious old daddy!" she laughed. "I believe you regard it as a sort of talisman. If you take it you'll only get it lost or stolen." "That's what I'm afraid of." His eyes roved towards the safe where the little gold god reposed, with its red eyes that glinted like sparks pf fire. "It's worth a good deal, you know, Cecilia." "To say nothing of the superstitious value you attach to it—oh, yes, you do, daddy." He pinched her arm. "Be quiet, Miss Im- pudence. Well, I shall decide to leave it behind—no doubt the safe's the best place for it. I'm not afraid of burglars, and no- body understaiols the mechanism of the lock but me."

" I do, and so does Philip," said Cecilia. " Ugh ! I hate the ugly god—l'm jealous of it. I know you like it better than your one and only daughter." "Look here, Cecilia darling, be a sensiblo little girl while I'm away. Of course you will have Aunt Martha to keep you in order, but understand there's to be no corresponding with that paintel• chap." "We only writs once a month as you said we might. I love him—yes, I do, dear—and I'll never love anyone else."

Tex months of the period of probation had email), passed, and Adrian`s last letter was quite pathetic. He had only sold one pict•ire, and that for much less than it was worth (of course !), and was in debt because he tried to economise by not paying his bills. He even went vegetarian for three weeks—sixpenny dinners masquerading as devilled turkey, stuffed goose, and so on, and all tasting much the same. But the only apprec'able result was that he got very thin and very hungry, and returned to the flesh-pots of Egnt when on the verge of starvation. Well, the end of it was Cecilia would marry her cousin and he —Adrian—would takc to drink and go to the dogs. Cecilia wept 'over the letter and kissed Adrian's latest photograph, in which he looked particularly beautiful and unworldly. What a terrible affliction to have a hard- hearted father to whom money, rather than genius, appealed! In two months tlis time would be up and her father would be home, and then—then---. Oh, it was too dreadful that their lives should be blighted for. the sake of a few hundred pounds! Even that wretched little gold god cost more than the sum which would make them happy for ever. Philip had rather a rough time of it with his cousin during these days. But he bore uer snubs and small tempers with the same good humour he had always shewn in the pinafore period when Cecilia tried to quarrel with him.

A fortnight before Mr. Faversham's return Blairton Towers was thrown into confusion. When the housemaid entered the library be- fore breakfast to draw up the blinds she found the window open, muddy marks on the carpet, and the room littered with papers. The safe had been forced open and the little gold god was gone! When the village constable arrived he made an examination, then scratched his head and looked wise. Philip watched him impatiently, while Cecilia stood near with whitening lips. "Well, sir, if you ask me, this 'ere job's been worked from the hinside of the 'ouse. Is there ,anything else missing besides the hidol?"

"No, the various papers and documents in the safe were untouched," said Philip. "The god was one greatly prized by my -uneie, and, worth, roughly speaking, three or four hun- dred pounds. But as to your suggestion, *I be- lieve the servants to be perfectly honest." "Begging your pardon, sir, I can tell from the marks on the window as it was hopened from the hinside, and the mud-marks on the carpet are just to throw dust in our eyes." The village-Constable was rapidly rivalling Sherlock Holmes.

"You see, there is a pretence of forcing the sate," he went on.

"A pretence ! " said Philip. "It has been done."

"Begging your pardon again, sir, the lock is a numerical one and was opened by some- body who was in the secret of its mechanism. Them scratches on the safe was done for the purpose, but Barnabas Brown ain't easily fooled. If the servants are as honest as you think they won't object to being questioned under the circumstances." The servants were called into the library and Philip explained what had happened. doing his best to avoid hurting their feelings. They at once offered to have their boxes searched, taking the matter in good part with the exception of Jenkins, the new par- lourmaid.

"I don't see why we should be disturbed in this way," she declared indignantly. "People had better be careful before they try to take away a honest girl's character. There is them as is himplicated more than us. Who was that gentleman, I'd like to know, as Miss Cecilia met in the grounds last night?" Cecilia flushed and then went pale under the scrutiny of the various eyes fixed upon her.

"What does Jenkins mean?" asked Aunt Martha, an extremely maiden lady of fifty summers.

"Jenkins is very—ixnpertinent," said Ceel- lia, "and I shall certainly not answer your question here, Aunt Martha." The servants looked on, immensely enjoy- ing the scene. Philip turned quietly to the police - constable and handed him several bunches of keys. _ _ _ .

" You had -better search the boxes, now, Brown, though I do not believe you will find the thief in this house." The search was drawn blank and Brown scratched his head thoughtfully. "I suppose you will offer a reward, sir?" "Yes, we must do that," said Philip, hesi- tating a moment. "Of course, we shall leave no stone unturned to get the god back. But the chances are that the thief will pick out the ruby eyes to sell separately, and put the gold in the melting-pot.' "No, sir, I don't think so. The gold god is worth double intact to what it would be otherwise, being what we might term bricky- brick. A look-out must be kept on the Lon- don curio and pawnshops—Blairton being within easy reach, I don't mind betting that the god will be found there." "The question is, where is it now?" said Philip. " Lor' bless you, sir, there are any amount of hiding-places about the house and in the grounds. I still 'old to the hopinion that the thief's hinside, though I've no further proof as you may say. Take my advice, sir, and put 'tec on.'

"Well, there's a vacancy for an under- footman—you can put in a plain clothes' man if you think he will be of any use." "Very good, sir. He'll keep an eye on the servants without their suspecting." Nobody at Blairton Towers felt like set- tling down to the ordinary daily routine after the morning's startling events. As Philip sat writing in the libz.ty-Cecilia came softly in.

"Are you wriiting to tell father?" she 'asked.

"No, dear; there is no need to worry him yet when perhaps the god will be found be- fore he returns.

"What did Brown say? Does he suspect anybody?" she questioned quickly, watching him with wide, frightened blue eyes. A sudden 'horrible suspicion gripped Philip. He took hold of her hands and found them cold and trembling. • "Cecilia, dear, I'm a stupid, blundering old :Amp, but I'd do anything in the world for my little cousin. If yo tr are worried let me help you. I will never betray your confidence I swear."

"What do you mean?" She snatched her hands angrily away. "Was that—at least, I mean did you meet Graham yesterday?" Philip asked rathet lamely. _ _

"How dare you question me? I won't tell you. Adrian is in London. Are you and Jen- kins in league to pry upon my movements?" And with this parting thrust Cecilia ran out of the room. The soi-disant under-footman arrived -latei in the day, but by the end of the week no fresh .discovery _had. been made. . .

Cecilia seemed to havo lost interest in every- thing unless it was the postman's visits. She looked a little ghost of a girl, and Aunt Martha recommended quinine and iron. They all dreaded Mr. Faversham's return—an event which otherwise would have been eagerly an- ticipated. But what would he say when he learnt the gold god was lost? One day Philip ran up tOtown on business, and when this was transacted more or less satisfactorily he strolled round till his train was due. "Why, it's not worth five pounds. Are you all mad?" They gazed at him with horror in their faces and Cecilia crept closer to Philip. Mr. Faversham hurriedly unstrapped his kit-bag and fished out a suit of pyjamas, which he carefully unrolled. And there lay the little gold sod, blinking and winking at them with it's wicked red eyes! "I decided to take it with me, after all," he explained. "Now perhaps you will tell me what this joke of yours is about?" "Oh," cried Cecilia, "I thought Adrian -" she caught herself up. "Oh," said Phillip, "I thought-" Then he, too, stopped.

And, "Oh," said Aunt Martha, "the dreadful scare that wretched gold god gave us!" Cecilia looked at Philip with eyes like droWned violets.

"I know—l understand—" she began. "What?" asked Mr. Faversham.

"That Philip is—is a darling and I lovo him!" Then she held out both hands to bim.

Philip stooped and kissed her. [Tlis END j

ROWDY WIDNES YOUTH.

At Widnes, on Monday, William Hughes, a young fellow of no fixed abode, stepped into the dock to answer four charges—drunk and disorderly, damaging constable's helmet (value ss. 9d.), breaking four panes of glass, and with assaulting the constable, on Saturday night. Police-constable Wrigley stated that at eight o'clock on Saturday night he was informed that a man was drunk in Church-street. He proceeded to the scene, and there saw prisoner behaving in a disorderly manner. Upon his arrest he knocked the constable's helmet off, and kicked it on the floor. On the way to the Folice station, he further assaulted him by tripping him and biting his hand. Prisoner pleaded guilty. The bench imposed a fine of ‘E2 15s. 9d., or one month,for the four charges brought against him.

Warrington and District Bowling Association. On Saturday evening the distribution of the cup and prizes, in connection with the above association, took place at the Cemetery Hotel. Mr. George Ball, jun., presided. A telegram was read from the Presi- dent (Mr A. H. Crosfield, ALP.) regretting his inability to be present and wishing success to the association. The Chairman, in proposing the health of the Pre -i- -dent, testified to the excellent work done by Mr. Crosfield in developing sport in Warrington. He claimed that Mr. Crosfield was held as a pioneer of bowls in Warrington. and that his efforts had been spread over Lancashire and Cheshire with unbounded success. The toast was drunk with the singing of "He's a jolly good fellow."—The Chairman presented the silver cup, given by the President, to the winning team, Bull's Head Bowling Club, and prizes to the runners up - Rylands Recreation.-.- A presentation of a pipe and case was made to Mr. G. F. Freeman (the hon. treasurer), and the Chairman remarked that as long as he was treasurer their finances would be well looked after and soundly administered. —Mr. Freeman thanked the members, and said he would be pleased to place his services at their disposal as he considered the game of bowls to be such a splendid recreation and one worthy of furthering for *ha benefit of his fellowmen. —Songs were given by Messrs. T. Humphreys, W. H. Eyes, J. W. Wrench. George Holbrook, R. Fairbrother, and George Ball. Mr. Wolfenden acted as pianist.

The Examilier, Saturday, November 16, 1907

THE MAYORALTY.

RE-ELECTION OF ALDERMAN SMETHURST.

Councillor Ellison Elected Alderman.

LIVELY DEBATE ON THE PRECEDENCE QUESTION.

Alderman James Smethurst was formally re-elected Mayor of Warrington for a second year at a meeting of the Town Council held at noon on Saturday. There were present :—The Mayor (Alderman Smethurst), the Deputy Mayor (Alderman Bolton). Aldermen A. Bennett, John Evans, W. J. Forshaw, W. Pierpoint, D. Tinnion, Councillors T. H. Ashcroft, J. Ball, T. Barton, W. Benson, T. Burton, T. Chantler, E, Cunnington, J. Dickenson, James Evans, L. Ellison, J. Hawthorn, Dr. Joseph,J. T. Locker, J. Lord, J. J. Nield, J. Pemberton, W. B. Roberts, W. Rogers, J. Sankey, J. Skelland. T. S. Steel, J. Fulton-Smith, F. Stott,O. Toole, J. Wilkinson, W. J. Wilson, Messrs. J. L. Whittle (town clerk), A. T. Hallway (deputy town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), T. Longdin (borough surveyor), J. Deas (water engineer), J. M. Murray (director of education), F. G. Ruddock (borough analyst), M. Nicholls (chief constable.) There was a large attendance of the public. The returns of the recent elections of Councillors having been read, the Mayor vacat€d the chair and the Deputy-Mayor was appointed to preside during the election of Mayor. ELECTION OF MAYOR.

Alderman BENNETT moved the re-election of Alder- man Smethurst as Mayor and spoke very highly of the way in which he had discharged his duties. They were all familiar with Alderman Smethurst's genial nature and that element of human kindness which made him not only liked but beloved of all who were privileged to enjoy his intimate acquaintance. One might well apply to him the words of the poet _

- "And thus he bore without abuse The grand old name of gentlemen." I Party feelings bad sometimes run high in the Council and they had had heated moments, but on no single occasion did he remember Alderman Smethnrst acting in any other than an impartial, genial and fair-minded spirit. Alderman Smethurst was also entitled to high honour for his long public services. He had been most assiduous in his attention to the work of the Council and of the committees. His ungrudging and hard work in the town generally had merited and won the grittitude not only of the Council but of all his fellow townsfolk. He was devotedly attached to the town. He (Alderman Bennett) believed the best thing in the world was to be born a Waringtonian. (Laughter.) The Mayor was a native of Bolton but he had become a Warringtonian—in fact he was more a Warringtonian than some people who bad been born in the town. He (Alderman Bennett) did not think they had ever had a Mayor who had given more public satisfaction or been more generally popular than Alder- man Smethurst. He had been admirably supported by his good wife who had been untiring in supplementing his labours for the good of the community. (Applause.) The same applied to every member of the family. In taking up the duties for a second year he believed Alderman Smethurst could count upon that unanimous snnnort of the Council which he thoroughly deserved. (Hear, hear.) Councillor TOOLE seconded. He had known Alder- man Stnethurst and his family for more than twenty- five years—in fact they were close neighbours for a long time. He was pleased to say that Alderman Smethurst had taken a kindly interest in his welfare and it gave him peculiar pleasure to second the resolution. Alderman TINNION supported. They could, be said, look upon the past year of the Council under Alderman Smethurst's Mayoralty with pleasure and pride. Some of them at the commencement had misgivings that Alderman Stnethurst's kindly nature and his very highly strung feelings might in some way operate against him, but he had more than proved the very sound judgment the Council exercised in electing him by his conduct in the chair. (Hear, hear.) He (Alderman Tinnion) felt sure that every member of the Co'uncil would admit that the services rendered by Alderman Smethurst had been marked by such dignity and kindness that it was always a pleasure to sit under his presidency. He had the hearty and unanimous support of the Council and he (Alder- man Tinnion) was sure that would he continued. (Hear, bear.) Speaking for the party which he had the honour to represent—Alderman Pierpoint to whom he always gave way not having chosen to speak at that juncture— he could assure the Mayor that be would receive their cordial support. Alderman Tinnion made a sympathetic reference to the Mayoress, who was absent through indisposition, and said they all hoped she would ,soon be restored to health and be able to give her husband that loving help that he had always so manfully acknow- ledged in that Council and elsewhere. He hoped the coming year would be a successful one for Warrington and that at the close the affection and esteem in which Alderman Smethurst was held would not have depre- ciated but increased. (Applause.) Councillor JAMES EVANS endorsed all that had been said and emphasised Alderman Tinnion's kindly refer- ence to the Mayoress. He hoped the coming year would be a very pleasant, happy and successful one. (Hear, hear.) The resolution was put to the meeting and carried with acclammation. THE MAYOR'S SPEECH. THE WORK OF THE YEAR.

The MAYOR was then acquainted with the decision of the Council and having taken the chair said :—When I accepted the office 12 months ago, 1 must confess I had grave misgivings as to my ability to discharge the duties to your satisfaction, but I am pleased to regard your action in re-electing me as an expression of approval of my services. May I briefly refer to a few of the important events of the past twelve months? Perhaps the most important is the settlement of the dreary action against the Manchester Ship Canal Company, which resulted in the vindication by the House of Lords of the claims of the Corporation and trades of the borough, and so decisive was the verdict that I think it scarcely likely that any further dispute between the Corporation and the Canal Company can at any time arise. (Applause.) I am also glad to believe that the less important action of the same company against the Corporation which has been referred to as "the battle of the sluices" is very likely to be satisfactorily settled, and an understanding arrived at, which will,l hope, prevent any further litiga- tion. The action which Colonel Edelsten had commenced against the Corporation has also been satisfactorily settled. The arbitration proceedings in connection with the claim of Mr. George Lawson were completed on Thurs- day last. I am glad,therefore, to think that I shall com- mence my second year of office with practically a clean sheet in so far as all matters of a litigious character have been disposed of. (Applause.) With regard to the widening of Bridge-street, I am pleased to say that terms have been amicably arranged with all owners and tenants of property in that street, and I hope before many months are over that the improvement will be completed. It is only fitting to refer to the memorial which we have erected during the year to the memory of the valiant services of the South Lancashire Regiment during the South African War, which was unveiled by General Sir Redvers Buller, V.C., early in the year. The year completed has also seen the opening of the first Council School and the laying of the foundation stone of another. The burning question of the hour is cer- tainly the one with reference to the proposed new bridge across the Mersey. I have personally given a good deal of attention to the matter during the last 12 months, and I feel somewhat impatient at what appears to be the unnecessary difficulties and delay which stand in the way of carrying out such a desirable improvement. We have not yet given up all hope. The cantilever scheme, which was in the first place vetoed by the Acting Conservator of the Mersey, will again be presented to that gentleman in a form which will receive his most careful consideration, and probably his approval. Further plans have been prepared with this object in view, and I can only express the hope that they will receive the approval of those to whom we are bound to submit them. If, however, we should be unsuccessful, then I hope the Council will face the question boldly, and go in for a new bridge. (Hear. hear.) I also hope that the Council will seriously consider the miestion of providing a new bridge across the Mersey at, Howley. (Hear, hear.) I know of no scheme which would be of so much direct advantage to the town. It will not only open up Victoria Park for the use and advantage of the inhabitants of the Howley district, hut will bring into close communication those plias of the f.own which are now so widely Emulated by the river. A new bridge at Howley would greatly relieve the traffic over War- rington Bridge, which is growing day by day more con- gested. There are many other matters which require attention during the year. The sanitary condition of the town will continue to receive the earnest consider- ation of the Health Committee, and the question of ; slum dwellings and the emission of black smoke— (laughter)—will not be lost sight of. The provision of a public abattoir is now engaging the attention of a special committee. Death has removed from our midst several familiar faces. I regret also to find that we have , lost two old friends through ill-health. Alderman Francomb, who has been a member of this Council for the last 27 years, and who was indeed, the "Father of the Council" and a most nseful member has been compelled to sever his connection with us,and for the same reqson, Mr. David Duncan, who has been a member of the Council for the past 12 years,also severs his connection with us. I feel sure that you join with me in an expression of appreciation of the valuable services they have rendered to the town, and in the hope that they ram soon be restored to fall health. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Our actions and debates have been subjected to much criticism in the past. I am not complaining nor do T think the Council will complain of fair criticism. f feel sure that the Council has done its best, and so long as it is criticised in the right spirit, and free from party or personal animus, Ido not think we would object, but rather welcome it. I would just say this to a few of our critics: If they think they could do better, let I' them come and show us how it can be done—(applause)--.

===== 0002601_19071116_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
and if they are not prepared to give us their help, then they should stop their fault-finding. There is an old saying that " the looker-on sees most of the game," but this is not true as regards municipal work. The Council meetinas seldom if ever, disclose the hard and serious work done in committees, where, in considering the interests of the town, every member I feel sure strives to arrive at the wisest and beet decision. I again thank you for thA honour you have for the second time conferred upon we, and I can only repeat what I said on a former occasion that as far as my health and abilities will allow, I will endeavour to discharge the duties of my high office not only to your satisfaction but to the satisfaction of the town. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19071116_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION OF ALDERMEN.

The next business was the election of five aldermen in place of Aldermen W. Bolton, W. J. Forshaw, J. Evans and J. Smethurst whose term of office expired and Alderman Francomb who had resigned through ill- health. Btllot papers were handed round and the voting resulted as follows :—William Bolton, 27 ; James Smethurst, 27; John Evans, 24; W. J. Forshaw, 24; Luke Ellison, 20; Thomas Burton, 5; John Wilkin- son, 2 ; James Evans, 2. Messrs. When the standing orders came up for confirmation, a discussion ensued on the question of precedence. The MAYoR, moved and Councillor FULTON-SMITH seconded the adoption of the standing orders as submitted. _

Alderman PIERPOINT moved an amendment that the standing order relating to procedene,e be not confirmed and that the Council revert to the old rule. The stand- ing order as submitted read :—" For the purpose of determining the seniority of members of the Council. with a view to the allotment of their seats in the Council Chamber, and for other purposes a list shall be prepared by the Town Clerk, in which shall be inserted in the first place the names of the several aldermen in the order of their respective periods of continuous and uninterrupted tenure of office, and in the next place the names of the several councillors in order of their respective periods of continuous and uninterrupted tenure of the office of councillor. but any interval not exceeding three months in duration shall not be con- sidered to be an interruption, provided that the Deputy Mayor shall in all cases take priority next after the Mayor." He said there was & peculiar fitness in his moving the amendment because the original standing order was altered on his account and he saw no reason why it should not be altered again. It had avoided useless bickering an 4 he believed it would do so in the future. Alderman EVANS seconded.

Alderman BOLTON was of opinion that the whole question should be re-considered and another standing order which would meet the general wishes of the Council, drawn up. Councillor Dr. JOSEPH said there appeared to be too much of the idea that seniority was the only qualifica- tion, and he did not agree with it. It was all right as far as it went, other things being equal. Councillor WILKINSON said be brought the matter forward at the caucus meetingand he had had to submit to a certain amount of abuse from all parties. The discussion had all seemei to turn on the wrong hinge. A paper was issued at the caucus meeting on which was placed the name of Councillor Rogers who had been three years out of the Council and who claimed that he took his old order of seniority. He (Councillor Wilkinson) believed that if a man had been out of the Council three years he should not take his old place. He further contended that there was no ground for placing the construction he had mentioned on the old Standing Order, which simply said that a list of the Council should be kept. He was prepared to modify the order so that absence from the Council of not more than twelve months should not be considered an interrup- tion. .

Councillor ROGERS said he was surprised to hear Councillor Wilkinson introduce the matter in the way he had done. Councillor Wilkinson was a member of the Council when the original order was rescinded, and the addition was made in order tbat when a man who had been serving the ratepayers was rejected but was ultimately again returned he should have his former ser- vce taken into account in determining seniority. He (Councillor Rogers) stood on a par with Alderman Pierpoint, for whom he had the greatest respect. The Alderman was in the Council nine years and was thrown out. He (Councillor Rogers) was in just the same position. The Alderman remained outside three years and so did he (Councillor Rogers.) The two cases were therefore on all fours. When there was an aldermanic vacancy looming in the distance an alteration was made and he supported it loyally. Councillor LORD said he was surprised that Coun- cillor Wilkinson had raised the point, as he believed he voted for the other Standing Order when it came before the Council. It was evidently a personal matter and he failed to see any logic iu Councillor Wilkinson's argument. He (Councillor Lord) could not blow hot for one and cold for another. DALTON ON BRIDGE BLUNDERS.

Everybody who plays bridge regularly is bound to make mistakes. That is an absolute certainty. Do not run away with the idea that the faculty of making blunders is confined, to indifferant players. Nobody is perfect at anything. We are told that even Homer nodded at times, and in the same way even the finest bridge-players will occasionally be caught napping. The only difference is that when the good player has made a blunder he will generally be the first to recog- nise the fact and to acknowledge it, whereas the indifferent player will try to defend himself and to argue that he was right. There are certain common and rather expensive errors which inexperienced players are very apt to fall into. Perhaps the most common of them all is not returning their partner's original lead at No Trumps because they can see a winning card, or possibly a tenace, in the dummy. They fail to recognise that that winning card or that tenses is there and is bound to make in any case, whether they lead up to it or not, so instead of continuing with the original slit they t)p,r) another one at random, and the result is generally disastrous. _

There can be no more aggravating partner than the player who will not return your original lead at No Trumps. He will say afterwards," I could • not return your lead right up to the ace or the king." But why not? That ace or that king is going to win a trick, and the sooner it is got rid of the better. A similar instance is when a player has to lead up to dummy and holds king and one other of a suit of which neither the ace nor the queen is in the dummy hand. Nothing will induce the indifferent player to' touch this suit. He will lead anything rather. He will cling on like grim death to that singly guarded king although it is absolutely useless unless his partner has I either the ace or queen. This lead of the king, from king and one, up to weakness in dummy is a very favourite one with all good players, especially against a suit declaration. It can do no possible harm, and it may be so very useful. I remember once leading from this combination up to the knave and two others in dummy. My king won the trick and I followed with the small one; my partner won with the queen and led another small one, which 1 trumped. The dealer had the ace all the time, but he had tried to play what is known as the " Bath coup," with the result that he did , not win a trick in the suit at all.

Some players are very wooden. No long ago I was playing with a partner of this pattern against a No-Trump declaration made by the dummy. My partner had the opening lead, and things had gone very badly for us— in fact, we had not won a single trick. At last he got the lead. Every suit was marked against us except spades, which had not been touched. My partner had king and one other, and the queen, ten, and another

were in dummy. The game was absolutely lost unless I held the ace and knave of spades. As a matter of fact, I had them both and three others, but do you suppose that he would lead that king of spades? Not a chance. He led something else, and we lost the game. When it was over I suggested, very mildly, "Could not you have tried me with a spade? It was the only chance of saving the game ?" He replied, in a most aggrieved tone of voice, "I couldn't possibly lead that. I had only king and one other, and I was certain to make a trick in the snit if I sat tight with it." He did sit tight with it, and we made one trick in the suit, but that was all we did make; and we lost five by cards instead of losing only the odd trick. Yet nothing on earth would have induced that man to acknowledge that he had played wrong.—From the "Strand Magazine," for November. THE COLLECTING oF OLD CLOCKS.

Somehow or other, the taste for old clocks frequently develops into a craze, and the note books of the curious contain many queer stories of clock collectors. I have heard of the descendant of a world-famous hero whose palace in Florence is crowded with clocks, the result of years of patient seeking. But during these long years of devotion to his hobby this eccentric became nervous and easily disturbed. The noise of his clocks, once music in his ears, became intolerable, and to avoid the possibility of further annoyance he caused the works to be entirely removed—so that the owner might be said to have become a collector of clock cases rather than clocks. Very frequently the outward part is that which attracts the modern collector, especially in regard to the tall examples of English work of a hundred and fifty years ago. The fine specimens of sixteenth-century European work have always had the advantage of being carefully protected for the benefit of futare generations. The extraordinary astronomical clocks of Germany and Northern Europe in the seventeenth century, and the elaborate and freakish productions of French and French Swiss workmanship during the next hundred years, are still to be found in wonderful preservation in public and private collections. Windsor Castle, to go no further afield, is known to have a number of specimens of eighteenth-century workmanship not easily matched in any part of the world. This period, indeed, is one of the most attractive to the amateur. The sixteenth-century work is historically extremely interesting. but not in keeping with the spirit of our time. The highly ecclesiastical character of the portable timekeepers of that day makes them more suitable for the museum than for domestic use, and thus we turn to the eighteenth century pro- ductions in this branch of applied art, as in most others, for those examples which are at once useful and decora- tive. In France and England especially, at that time, admirable and lasting work was produced, specimens of which fit with easy grace into our more or less modern surroundings.—Egan new in the November "Pall Mall Magazine."

IN THE HEART OF THE STORM.

The Captain was a curiously devil-may-care sort of man, sid I found that he had brought me down to explalli thsit the_vortex would be upon us very soon, and that I should have the chance of a lifetime to get a snap of the much-talked-of pyramidal sea. And, in short, that he wished me to have everything prepared, and the pistol ready loaded with flashlight powder ; for, as he remarked: "If we get through, it'll be a rare curiosity to show some of those unbelieving devils ashore.' In a little we had everything ready, and then we made our way once more up on deck, the Captain placing the pistol in the pocket of his silk oil-skin coat. And above us and around us, filling all the sky with sound, was the incessant mad yowling of th 3 cyclone the noise so vast, and the volume and mass of the wind so enormous that I am impressed now, looking back upon the scene, with a sense of having been in a semi- stunned condition through those last minutes. I was conscious now that a vague time passed. A time of noise, and wetness, and lethargy. Then, abruptly, a tremendous flash of lightning burst through the clouds. It was followed almost directly by another, which seemed to rive the aky apart. Then, so quickly that the succeeding thunderclap was audible to our wind-defeaned ears, the wind ceased, and, in the com- parative, but hideously unnatural silence, I caught the Captain's voice shouting : "The Vortex—quick ! " Even as I pointed my camera over the rail, and opened the shutter, my brain was working with a preter- natural avidity, drinking in a thousand uncanny sounds and echoes that seemed to come upon me from every quarter, brutally distinct against the background of the cyclone's receding howling. There were the harsh, bursting, frightening, intermittent noises of the seas, and, mingling with these, the shrill, hissing scream of the foam ; the dismal sounds, that suggested dankness' of water swirling over our decks, and the faintly-heard creaking of the gear and shattered spars; and then— flash, in the same instant in which I had taken in these varied impressions, the captain had fired the pistol, and I' saw the Pyramidal Sea —a sight never to be forgotten, a sight rather for the dead than the living, a sea such as I could never have imagined, boiling and bursting upward in monstrous clots of water and foam as big as houses. I heard, without knowing 1 beard, the Captain's expression of amazement. Then a thunderous roar in my ears. One of those vast, flying hills of water had N.ruck the ship.—From "Through the Vortex of a Cyclone " in the" Cornhill Magazine" for November.

THE GROWTH OF SOUTH AFRICA.

Darine. the late war it was claimed for the Africanders that they were, in South Africa, on equal terms with ourselves, but facts tell another tale. When the British took over the Cape Colony it was limited to the coast districts, and its white population numbered less than 30.000. In a, slightly longer period the colonies founded by England in North America developed into a nation of 4,000,009 strong. Or compare the growth of South Africa during the century of British rule, and the growth of South Africa during the preceding century and a half of Dutch rule. The trading station of 1800 is, in 1903, an empire stretching from Lion's Head in the south to the Equator in the north, and its white population is three-quarters of a million. The British made South Africa, not the Dutch, who, in the main, have always been unprogressive, whether in the Cape Colony or the Transvaal. From an interesting article on "The Naval Bases of the Empire," which appears in the November "Windsor Ntagazine," a notable number containing complete stories by Eden Phillpotts. Robert Barr, Baroness von Hutton, Barry Pain, Mary E. Mann and other favourite novelists. "Some Country Problems and their Solution " is the title of an interesting article illus- trated from very fine photographs, and the new article in the series on modern artistes recalls the work of the late J. B. Burgess, R.A., with seventeen admirable reproductions from his best pictures. Mr. J. A. Fuller naitland, the well-known authority on matters musical. contributes an interesting article on The Art of.the Song-Cycle" and Herr von Warlich, the singer. Charles G. D. Roberts is represented by fx characteristic story of animal life; and many other stories and articles make up a number of remarkable variety and distinction. THE R.P.A. ANNUAL.

The Rationalist Press Association is this year issuing "The Agnostic Annual" under the title of "The R.P.A. Annual." To the first number of the new series the Hon. John Collier, the eminent artist whose pictures are so well known end so widely appreciated by fre- quenters of the Royal Academy. contributesan interest- ing paper on The Home Life of Thomas Henry Huxley." Mr. Collier lived for some years with the Huxleys after his marriage to Miss Huxley, and be thus had exceptional opportunities of becoming acquainted with the domestic life of the Professor. Among the other writers in the Annual are Mr. Leonard Huxley, who discourses on "Some Ethics and a Moral;" Mr. Robert Blatchford, the.editor of the "Clarion," who explains how he became an Agnostic; Mr. Eden Phillpotts, the well-known novelist, who chants a" A Song of a Weary World ;" Mr. Alfred W. Benn, Mr. John M. Robertson M.P., and the Rev. A. W. Hatton.

SOME of the newspapers SOCIALISM, report certain remarks of "OF SORTS." Bishop GORE under the heading of " a Bishop's Socialism." What the Bishop of Birmingham appears to have said is that the whole of our industrial system requires profound modification. "There is now," he added, "a movement of the waters, an awakening of the,mass of the people to secure better conditions and more equal opplr- tunity." Nothing in the world, he went on to say, ought to stir the imagination and excite the sym- pathy of Christians so much as this movement for fundamental reform of social conditions. These are most excellent sentiments, but they are as far removed from any views that could properly be called Socialistic as they could well be, It is time that a protest were raised against the loose use of words in what is becoming one of the great con- troversies of the day. There is no necessary con- nection between the word " social " and Socialism or between social well-being and that state of society which Socialism would establish. When a Bishop argues in favour of equality of opportunity he is not preaching Socialism, but quoting Herbert Spencer, who was an individualist of the straitest sect.

We all of us agree—even the possessors of wealth must agree—that it would be a good thing if the masses of the people could secure better conditions of life, and if the industrial system could be so modified as to secure a more even and equitable distribution of the earnings of capital and labour. It is the ai:n of Liberalism to secure better conditions of existence and more equal opportunities. That is the old doctrine. Thera is absolutely nothing novel in what Bishop GORE has been saying at Birmingham. He has merely been proving once more that outside the affairs of the Church be is a man of sound and progressive Liberal views. We welcome him as a helper in the field of our own efforts, protesting against such a label being attached to him as is likely to bring odium on his views. The passion to advance humanity is not a thing of yesterday. It did not begin with Mr. HYNDMAN Or with Mr. KEIR HARDIE. It is in no way specially connected with Socialism, though most Socialists are, we believe, quite genuinely inspired by it. Such views on social conditions as those of Bishop GORE are really the soul of Christianity. If it were possible to get from the Socialists themselves a satisfying and decisive definition of Socialism, Bishop GORE would not only escape a wrong description, but the Conservative Central Agency, and all the less responsible part of the Conservative Press, would be at once defeated in the attempt to brand the Liberal Party as Socialistic. Socialists differ very widely as to what they want or would be willing to take under certain circumstances; yet, we submit, there is only one genuine brand of Socialism. The owner- ship in common of the land and all the means of production is what the true Slcialist is aiming at. To describe the acquisition by the community of the gasworks and the tramworks, then, as an advance towards Socialism is rank nonsense. It is merely applying to a certain mode of transit and to a universal method of public lighting the prin- ciple which from time immemorial has been applied to main roads and bridges. Socialistic doctrine may be good or bad. We do not feel called upon to pronounce judgment on the subject. What is clear is that any realisation of Socialistic ideals in the true sense is as remote as the Greek Kalends. In fact, all the social ideals of Bishop GORE might become realities without bringing us much nearer to Socialism.

In consequence of the A By-ELECTION, election of Councillor Ellison to the Alderrnanic vacancy there will have to be a by-election in Latchford Ward. Candidates are already in the fiel.:l, and the selection of Mr. W. Bibby by the Liberals, and Mr. H. Singleton by the Conservatives gives promise of a rousing party fight. There is one thing we can cordially congratulate the Conserva- tive nominee, Mr. Singleton, upon, and that is the fact that he is not connected with the drink trade. It is a distinction in these times for a Tory municipal candidate in Warrington to be some- thing other than a publican or a brewer. Mr. Bibby is eqwally free from the taint—Liberal publicans are rare—and he should make a suitable and acceptable successor to Alderman Ellison, who certainly looked well after the material welfare and comfort of the Ward. Both candidates go out of their way to allude to the question of religious teaching in elementary schools, which does not concern them as prospec- tive members of the Town Council. The people, and the State, decide what shall be the law on the matter, and local authorities merely have that law to administer. However, as Mr. Bibby and Mr. Singleton take substantially the same view of the question there should be no fear cf a sectarian split. Mr. Bibby will make a strong Liberal candidate—one who has resided for 25 years in the Ward—and we hope to see him returned.

===== 0002601_19071116_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
We have received the THE MEMBER, gratifying intimation this TO BE MARRIED. week that Mr. A. H.

Crosfield, M.P., the Borough Member, is shortly to be married. This will be good news to all his friends and constituents. Many people have thought that the one thing more than any other which Mr. Crosfield lacked was the saving grace of matri- mony. He has too long been in the restless state of single blessedness and we are unfeignedly glad that he has at last capitulated unreservedly to feminine charms in the person of Miss Elliadi, a daughter of Greece—the home of beauty and mythology. The date of the wedding has not yet been made public, but it cannot be far distant, for the Member expects to attend with his bride the Parr Hall meeting to be addressed by Mr. Birrell on December 18th. On behalf of Warrington Liberals we should like,even at this stage, to extend to Mr. Crosaeld the heartiest congratulations and good wishes.

Mr. Balfour's speech at MR. BALFOUR. Birmingham still leaves the _ _ _

nation In doubt as to how far he is prepared to go with the out and out Pro- tectionists, but in common with his party he now stands committed to Tariff Reform, and the issue should again be quite clear at the next General Election. With his usual ingenuity the Ex- Premier declared that he would not bind him se f against a tax on any article whatever so long as that tax dil not involve an additional burden upon the workingman." He says nothing definite in answer to repeated challenges about taxing corn,meat and butter, and the "Manchester Guardian "caustically remarks : "To give one's assent to food taxes while refusing one's assent to the infliction of hardship on the poor is like agreeing to throw a friend to the wolves in the amiable hope that wolves have given up biting since they were last heard of." Poor Mr. Balfour ! As an exponent of philosophic doubt he is admir- able, but to give a plain answer to a plain question involves hire hopelessly in a sea of perplexity.

The election of Mayor and ELECTION Cl' MAYOR Alder men was conducted AND ALDERMEN. with praiseworthy amia- bility and dispatch on Saturday. Alderman Smethurst was re-elected Mayor for a second year amid a chorus of praise, and the four retiring Aldermen, Messrs. Bolton, Evans, Forshaw, and Smethurst, were also re-appointed for a further term of six years. Th 3 vacancy caused by the retirement of Alderman Francomb had been the subject of much discus- sion during the week prior to the meeting, but there was very little cross-voting and Councillor Ellison succeeded to the position by the unanimous vote of the Tory and Liberal parties who were found, on this matter, to be in absolute and complete agreement. At the next meeting of the Council, there will probably be another vacancy among the Aldermanic chairs, for we should think, by that time, the Mayor will have decided to announce the retirement through ill-health of Alderman James Fairclough. Though various gentlemen were appointed to various Committees in the place of Alderman,Fairclough, no reference was made to the fact that he had resigned. In point of seniority, Councillor Burton is the next member on the list for elevation to the select ranks of the Aldermen. We are curious to note whether the same remarkable unanimity between the two political parties will be manifested in his favour also. We sincerely hope it will.

The question of precedence or seniority was settled on the lines we suggested in our last issue. Standing Order M, which we were surprised to see Councillor Wilkinson stood sponsor to, re-intro- duced the obnoxious clause relating to continuous and uninterrupted service. Alderm in Pierpoint, who has good reason to remember the time when such a rule was in force, moved an amendment deleting the stipulation about continuous service, and his amendment was carried by twenty-one votes to none. Councillors Wilkinson and Rogers, who are both interested parties in the interpreta- tion of seniority, spoke to the amendment with some warmth and as a result received a fatherly lecture on their bad taste from Alderman Tinnion, which was one of the funny incidents of the morn- ing. All the service a member of the Council has given to the Corporation in such capacity now counts in determining seniority, whether there is a break in his service or not. This is the only fair principle, and we are glad that the Council on re-consideration upset Standing Order 51 in the form it was thoughtlessly agreed upon at the caucus meering. It could not do otherwise and maintain the slightest shred of reputation for consistency.

The value of a. Museum THE MUSEUM AND and Library to any pro- LIBRARY. gressive community can hardly be over-estimated and there is every indication that our local institu- tion is fully appreciated. The annual report just issued by the curator and librebrian (Mr. C Madeley) affords some interesting information on this head. It appears that the total number of visitors to the Museum during the year was roughly estimated at 29,997. Parties in charge of teachers from some of the Elementary Schools were noticed occasionally, and visits by school children making notes for lessons have been more numerous than in former years, and judging from the greater frequency of applications for assistance in naming specimens, or for advice as to methods of dealing with injurious insects' it is fair to assume that serious interest in the Museum is on the increase. The issue of books from the Lending Library showed an increase of 6 per cent. over that of the pravious year, the total being 92,291. Fiction, of course, was most in demand, the pro- portion of works per cent. of this class issued, being:6l.B. Poetry and drama figure at 1 and theology at .6. It is pleasing to note that no fewer than 740 students' tickets were issued, the object being to encourage the reading of the more serious and useful books. As a result, we learn that the proportionate issue of every class of "non- fiction " was somewhat raised, and in particular there was a notable increase in the call for music. The work at the delivery stations opened in Church-street, Green-street, and Winwick-street showed a steady though not rapid rise, while the school library has met with encouraging succes., the issues to scholars for home readin4 amounting to 9,131.

It is curious to note that LIISITANIA'S NEXT the next record of the RECORD. Lusitania is likely to be contingent on an economy of time at the beginning of the voyage. It will not be in the last but in the first lap that she will win the next race. Her fast steaming from land to land has hitherto served no practical pur_ pose. It has produced talk of a shorter passage but for passengers the journey is not shortened On her two voyages she has reached Sandy Hook at about 1.15 and 230 on Friday morning re- spectively, and has perforce had to wait there for daylight before steaming up to harbour. Judged on her trial test, the Mauretania might even make Sandy Hook at 10.14 p.m. the night before, but that would not help matters. She would only wait three hours longer than her sister ship. Either of the great Cunarders, to give passenger 3 the benefit of her exceptional speed, must reach New York before dark on Thursday evening. That can only be done by accelerating the departure from the last land after leaving Liverpool outward bound. The Sunday morning mails which she now picks UP at Queenstown prevent her leaving Roche Point before nine or ten o'clock. With an earlier departure from Liverpool, and continuous steam- ing, she could gain nine hours in the initial stage. Somebody's Saturday afternoon letters would be left by a shipment of mails in the Mersey, but that is apparently the only solution of the problem of a shorter journey between landing-stage and landing-stage.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

%'°W;4,

Having agreed to the adoption of the Provision of Meals Act, the Education Committee will on Monday be asked to add the administration of that enactment to the powers and duties of the School Attendance Sub-Committee.

* * Success to the Warrington Musical Festival! The project has just been inaugurated with every prospect of a satisfactory issue and we hope Warrington will prove that it is capable of appreciating and generously supporting this effort for the advancement of musical culture in the locality. * o

Whitecross Ward is fortunate in having such a capable representative as Councillor Hawthorn who in his address to the Junior Liberals on Monday evening showed a thorough grasp of the questions which are occupying the attention of our local legislators. He views matters in a true progressive light and should have the support of his constitutents in pressing for improvements which make for the betterment of the lot of the poor and the commercial progress of Warrington.

Councillor W. B. Roberts earned distinction the moment he was elected by not returning thanks in the customary way from the Town Hall steps after the declaration of the poll. There was much comment oa this sin of omission, with the result that in a circular he send round the Ward, Councillor Roberts advances the remarkable explanation that" my sole reason for not doing so was that a suggestion was made by Mr. Clegg and his party that no speeches were to be made outside." Mr. Clegg denies flatly that he ever heard. of any such suggestion or arrangement, but Mr. Roberts refuses to withdraw. A thinner excuse than his we never heard. Why should he agree with any- body to refrain from thanking personally the electors who had placed him at the head of the poll? * * * The Rev. Thomas Miller, the newly-appointed minister to the Warrington Wesleyan Circuit, spoke in a humorous vein at Emmanuel Church sale of work on Thursday. He frankly remarked that the itinerary system had its advantages, as it enabled a preacher to deliver the same sermon on numerous occasions, but it had an undoubted drawback as a minister was liable to be called upon to take part in sales of work at three or four Churches which came under hie charge during his stay in one circuit. Mr. Miller made his first attempt at opening a sale, but all were agreed that he performed the duty exceedingly well, his geniality I especially commending him to the workers.

_ o o 0 The Empress Augusta Victoria, besides being the Consort of the King's nephew, is related to our Royal Family, both by blood and by marriage, in several other ways. Prince Christian is her uncle, the brother of her father, late Duke Frederick of Schleswig- Holstein. Her mother, the late Duchess Adelaide, a princess of Hohenlohe-Langenburg, was the niece of Queen Victoria, her mother being a princess of Leiningen, a daughter of the Duchess of Kent by her first husband, the Prince of Leiningen. The Empress's sister, the Duchess Carolina Matilda, is the wife of Duke Frederick Ferdinand of Scbleswig-Holstein- Sonderburg-Gliicksburg, a first cousin of Queen Alexandra. Duke Ernst Giintber of Schleswig-Holstein, the Kaiserin's brother, is married to the Princess Dorothea of Coburg, one of King Edward's Saxe- Coburg cousins, whose father was Prince Philip of Saxe-Coburg and whose mother the Princess Luise of Belgium. - _

• 50 The Junior Liberals at their debate last Honda y, reported in our columns, embraced the opportunity of voicing their opinions on certain " burning" questions affecting the citizens of the town. The information that several of them possessed certainly proved that not only were they " up-to-date " but alive to the importance of several improvements. Revelations were also made with respect to private slaughter houses, which fairly staggered some members of the audience, and a very strong case was made out for the necessity of a Public Abintoir. The Bridge Ques- tion too was handled in a most businesslike way, and some of the Junior Liberals showed that they under- stood finance and rates questions better than most ratepayers. It was demonstrated that a new bridge estimated to dost £20,000 could be erected at less than a id. in the £ increase on the rates.

* * * Mr. Lloyd George has been so successful in dealing with the dispute between the railway companies and their servants that it is quite natural that his attention should be directed to other parts of railway administra- tion. The suggestion, however, that he is going to attempt to regulate competition on the railways seems a little far fetched. He has no powers (says the London Correspondent of "The Livepool Post and Mercury ") which would enable him to interfere in this subject, and the directors of the companies after their recent experience may be left to see that railway competition is not conducted on ruinous conditions. It is, however, not unlikely that he may suggest an inquiry into the whole working of our railway system, especially as it bears on rates and competition. It is many years since there has been any inquiry into the question of rates and fares, and, so far as I am aware, there has been no investigation into the working of our railway system as a whole. In these circumstances, I should not be surprised if the President of the Board of Trade decided on a general inquiry into the whole of our system. _

o 0 o The whole community will be relieved to learn that the sudden illness of the Premier is passing away and there is every reason to believe that in a faw days he will be quite well again. His illness is mainly due to overwork. His labours during the last eight days would have been trying to a stronger and younger man. There were two Cabinet Councils last week. Then came the Guildhall banquet on Saturday, which put a considerable strain on the Prime Minister. On Monday he was at Windsor to meet the Kaiser. He returned to town on Tuesday for a Cabinet Council, and went again to Windsor in the evening for the State banquet. On Wednesday he was at the Lord Mayor's banquet to the German Emperor and Empress. From that he hurried off to Bristol, where in the even- ing he delivered a long and vigorous speech. This is a tale of work which few men could go threngli without severely taxing their strength, and it is no svonder that the Prime Minister should be suffering from the effect of it. He is going on very satsfac- torily, and it is expected that he will be able to return to town to-day or on Monday. * *

The scheme for the provision of a new school and recreation ground in Lovely-lane is making progress. Next Monday evening the Education Committee will be asked to approve the Sites and Buildings Com- mittee's recommendation that subject to the approval of the Board of Education,not less than three acres of land in the Lovely-lane site be purchased for the pur- pose of erecting thereon a public elementary school; and that the Town Council be recommended to pur- chase, conditionally upon the approval of the Board of Education being received, the site in Lovely-lane cot- taining seven acres, for the joint purposes of a public elementary school and a public playground. Subject to a reasonable price being arranged we believe the scheme is a sound one, and that it will be heartily welcomed by the inhabitants of the district. The existing school accommodation is being severely taxed and the recreation ground is 83 much appreciated by the youngsters who have for some time had the privilege of romping upon it that it would be a pity to let it fall into the hands of the builders.

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS ELECTION INCIDENT. ALLEGED BREACH OF THE LAW.

Mr. E. Darby, who was one of the recent candidates for South Wind's Ward of St. Helens has written to the Public Prosecutor enclosing cuttings from the papers of his speech in which he charged the two other candidates in tile election with feeding the police and their workers, contrary to law. Mr. Darby has received an acknowledgment from the Home Secretary, who states that the matter will have his attention. Mr. Darby says that he will press the matter forward, and expects to have the successful candidate unseated.

THE MAYORALTY.

The election of Alderman H. Martin was moved by Sir Christopher Gamble and seconded by Mr. Ellison. On being put to the vote all members yresent voted in favour except six Labour members, who, however, offered no opposition. The election of five aldermen followed, and Alderman Bishop, Sir Christopher Gamble, Colonel W. W. Pilkington, Colonel R. Pilking ton, and Alderman H. Martin were re-elected. Mr. J. Fisher, the oldest councillor, was the only other member nominated, and he received ten votes. The Mayor, in returning thanks for his election, said he relied on the support of all the members. He knew of nothing very heroic that was likely to occupy, their attention during the year. Efficiency with economy would be their watchword. He was glad to think that the trade of the towu and the country generally was in a fairly satisfactory condition. He would like to refer to the feeling of satisfaction throughout the country that the threatened disaster in the shape of a general railway strike had been happily averted. They would never know the exact extent of what they owed to his Majesty the King for what he had done in the matter, as he had done in connection with international matters, but they were all greatly indebted to his Majesty and his advisers for the course they had taken in bringing about a settlement which seemed satisfactory to all the parties concerned. (Applause.) Alderman C. J. Bishop was appointed Deputy-Mayor. Mr. Forshaw objected to Alderman Beecham being re-elected on the Electricity Committee, and moved that Mr. Glynn should take his place. He hoped that Alderman Beecham would not misconstrue the remarks he was going to make, but he felt that, whatever abilities Alderman Beecham had, it would be better for the government of the town if an outlet was found for them on the Electricity Committee. He said this because it was generally known that Alderman Beecham was largely interested in the South Lancashire Tram- ways Company, and some time ago Lord Stanley informed the shareholders of that company that they had practically a controlling interest in the St Helens Tramways. Everyone on the Electricity Com- mittee knew that they had continually important ques- tions coming up where the interests of the St. Helens Tramways Company directly clashed with the interests of the St. Helens Corporation, and from that standpoint he did not think Alderman Beecham should be chairman of the committee. For his own sake, it would be far better if he stayed off the Electricity Committee under the circumatances. Then there was the British Insulated hire Company, and he understood that Alderman Beecham was a shareholder of that company. Month by month they were giving contracts to lhat particular company, so that all things considered he thought it would be for the benefit of the town that Alderman Beecham should go off the committee.—Mr. Rudd seconded.—Alderman Beecham was apparently rising to reply when the Mayor put the amendment, which was lost, only the eight Labour members voting for it against seventeen on the other side.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

ALLEGED HOUSEBREAKING AT WIDNES.

At Widnes, on Monday, Fred Ford, of Ditton was charged with entering a dwelling-house in John-street, Widnes, on the 9th inst., and stealing a silver watch, 2s. 6d. in money, and 7s. in copper from the slot-meter, the property of Sarah Overton and the Widnes Corpora- tion. Mrs. Overton said she retired to bed after seeing the place secure, and when she got up next morning she found the whole of the drawers in the place upset, and the window open. Information was given to the police, and on Sunday Detective-sergeant Davidson went to Runcorn, and in a public house there saw the prisoner. Upon him were found the watch and money. A remand was granted for a week to enable the police to make further inquiries. BOARD OF GUARDIANS. A meeting of this Board was held at Dutton Work- house on Tuesday afternoon under the presidency of Mr. T. Baxter. There were also present :—Messrs. J. Hallows, G. Pilling, S. Horton R. Nixon, J. Spruce, J. Renshaw, W. Harrison, J. Bolshaw, E. Britch, J. Mynekyme, W. Winstanley, 0. E. Warburton, T. Whitlow, W. Weir, W. Turner, J. B. Chadwick, A. Broome, P. Booth, A. Haining, T. Antwis, C. E. Linaker, senior, J. H. Davies, T. Wright, El. Mawdsley, T. Guest, G. F. Ashton (clerk) and the relieving officers. LAW OF SETTLEMENT AND REMOVAL. The clerk stated that the Prestwich Union had forwarded the following resolution for approval: " That a communication be addressed to the Guardians of the Poor Law Unions in Lancashire, Yorkshire, Cheshire, Derbyshire and Staffordshire to the object of entering into an arrangement whereby the law of settle- ment and removal of paupers (other than lunatics) should as between those Unions and this Union be suspended." The letter was ordered to lie on the table. THE NURSING STAFF. On the minutes of the Visiting Committee Mr. William Morris moved that the opinion of the Local Government Board be obtained as to whether the Guardians could appoint a probationer to assist the present nurses and until this opinion is obtained the whole of the question of the nursing staff be adjourned. Mr. Spruce: This resolution arose from a joint letter sent by two nurses to the Visiting Committee at the last meeting. They don't seem to be satisfied. The resolution was passed.
