INSPECTION OF WIDNES WORKS. .

A letter Wa-s-r-e-a-d-if7or-n- Messrs. Gibbon and Arkle, of Castle-street Liverpool, with regard to the proposed appointment of another additional inspector under the Alkali Acts for the Widnes and Runcorn area. The Town Clerk of Liverpool suggested Thursday, Novem- ber 21st, at the Municipal Offices, Liverpool, as the date for a conference of the representative of the Urban and Rural Councils. The matter was left in the hands of the repre- sentatives to the conference, Messrs. Wright and Linaker, sen. AN APPOINTMENT.

Mr. A. W. Blain of Norley, was appointed clerk of the works for the 'Norley water scheme at a salary of 355. a week. SHOULD THE ROA.DNIEN HAVE HOLIDAYS. Mr. T. Haining said that as a rule all the men and women who worked for the Council got a holiday and were paid for it. He did not see why the roadrnen should not have a holiday and be paid for it. They were out in all weathers - Mr. S. Horton: They shelter in bad weather. Mr. Haining (contining) said they were deserving of a holiday and he moved "That all roadmen in the employ of the Council be allowed a holiday in the summer and that they be paid for such holiday." Mr. J. Hallows said that all men should be allowed a holiday and be paid for it. When a roadman wanted a day off he had to ask for it and also miss a day's pay, The other roadmen would gladly do one of the other men's work while he had a holiday, if they knew that it would come their turn to have a holiday later on. It would cost the Council nothing. If a man had a holiday he would go back to work "refreshed with wine.'" (Laughter.)He did not mean what the other members of the Council meant but the men would go to work in a good spirit. He seconded Mr. Haining's motion. Mr. T. Wright said that he had a resolution to put forward to the Council and that was "That roadmen be allowed to leave work on Saturdays at one o'clock." The roadmen went to dinner at twelve o'clock and had to come back and work till three- The Chairman: You are off the question and you will have to give notice of motion. Mr. C. E. Linaker said he rose to support Mr. Haining's motion because all men should be allowed a holiday. After further discussion the matter was left in the hands of the Highways Executive Committee.

THE WIDNES TRAGEDY. INQUEST AGAIN ADJOURNED. The Coroner ( Ar. F. A. Jones) held his adjourned inquest at the Widnes Police Court on Tuesday morning on the body bf Rose Ann Byrne' aged sixty years, who was found dead on the pavement outside her house at Brown-street,Moss Bank, Widnes,at six o'clock on Sunday morning, the 3rd inst. At first it was thought that it was a case of suicide, as the woman had previously attempted her own life by jumping out of the window. During the day certain information came to the know- ledge of the police, and her husband, John Byrne, an elderly salt-cake man, employed at Pilkington's works, was arrested on the charge of causing his wife's death. In answer to the charge he said, "1 know nothing about it, and I deny it altogether." Byrne, as he stepped in the dock, presented a haggard appearance. Mr. B. B. Peters has been engaged in his defence. After the jury had been sworn the coroner announced his intention of further adjourning the inquiry. If the evidence against Byrne was sufficient to justify them in sending him for trial, the Public Prosecutor would have to be notified ; and, seeing that the Liverpool Asgizes commenced on Wednesday, it was impossible to complete the inquest and the police inquiry in time for the presentation of the case to the grand jury. Under these circumstances it would be far better to adjourn the case. Tuesday next at 10.30 was fixed for the further hearing.

POLICE COURT REVELATIONS._

Mr. W. W. Gossage sat at the Widnes Policia Court,on Tuesday morning, when Byrne was brought up on remand charged with causing the death of Rose Ann Byrne. Police-constable Hill stated that he entered the house first. He tried to awaken the occupants, but as he could not he eventually burst the door open. He found Costello, the lodger, asleep on the sofa in the kitchen, and the prisoner upstairs in bed. He awakened him and he came downstairs. When he came downstairs he said What's this ?" Witness replied," Can't you see ? It is your wife, who has been found out in the street." 'I wondered where she had been," replied prisoner. "I was downstairs this morning to get a drink and went back to bed, and I must have fallen asleep again, and I did not know about this until :Ars. Wright told me." He afterwards looked at the body, and exclaimed "What will I do? Poor old Rosy !" Witness afterwards examined the front bedroom. The box on trestles was under the window, and with the exception of the perambulator the whole of the furniture was in the court. In the back bedroom he found the key of the front door on the table. There was an old iron bedstead and straw mattress, and some old rags as a covering. Prisoner's coat was hanging behind tllp door. Sergeafit Blackhall gave evidence of arrest, and after charging the prisoner at the police station, prisoner replied, That's a thing I deny altogether." At the request of the bench, a statement was given as made by prisoner. He left work at twenty past nine on Saturday night, the 2nd inst., and went for a drink. He subsequently saw his wife coming out of the "Golden Bowl. They went home together, and he retired to bed about ten o'clock. The next time he saw her was on Sunday morning, when he was knocked up by the police. The lodger, Thomas Costello, said he had been with the Byrnes five weeks. During that time he had slept downstairs on a sofa in the front kitchen. On Saturday he brought his money from the works and paid the deceased woman 15s. for his lodgings. When he returned to the house in the afternoon he was very drunk, and only just fancied seeing the policeman. He remembered being awakened the following morning by the prisoner coining downstairs for a drink. Ira reply to prisoner, witness said he had a gill of whisky. He told him to get a glass and he would give him some. Prisoner produced a basin, and had a drink of the whiskey and retired to bed. During the live weeks he had been lodging there he never remembered prisoner coming downstairs before, and only on that occasion ever saw a light in the back kitchen. Piisoner was remanded until next Tuesday.

ANNUAL SALE OF WORK.

The annual sale of work in connection with Emmanuel (Reformed Episcopal) Church was opened in the Church Institute, Knutsford-road, on Wednesday and continued until last night. The aggressive work which this church carries on is loyally and generously supported by the congregation but the obligations undertaken are so heavy that outside help has to be appealed for. It is with this object that the sale is held and the work of preparation is always entered upon with commendable energy. This year's sale was no exception, and as the result of the ladies' efforts there was a large array of useful and fancy wares. The stalls were presided over by the following : WoRK STALL (No. 1) : Mrs. Vaughan, Mrs. Hodge and friends.

WORK STILL (NO. 2) : Mrs. Annie Gandy and friends.

REFRESHMENT STALL: Mrs. Thomas Allen, Miss Lees and friends. CHRISTIAN ENDEAVOUR STALL: Miss Booth and friends.

HOUSEHOLD STALL: Miss S. Gandy and friends. FLOWERS AND FRITIT STALL : Mrs. W. Bibby and friends. TEA ROOM : Mrs. Elias Allen and friends. FISH POND: Miss Minnie Booth. OPENING BY MRS. DODDS.

The sale was opened on Wednesday afternoon by Mrs. James Dodds. The Rev. F. Vaughan presided over a • good attendance and was supported by Mr. D. Hodge • (minister's warden) and Mr. Thomas Allen (people's warden). The Rev. F. VAUGTIAN,On behalf of the congregation, extended a very cordial welcome to Mrs. Dodds. He said that Mrs. Dodds' personal connection with philanthropic and, indeed, all aggressive Christian work in the town and neighbourhood,was too well-known and highly appreciated to need any emphasis from him. They welcomed her for her own sake and for the sake of the work to which she had given her heart and her labour for so long. They at Emmanuel Church had again and again had the presence and help of Mr. Dodds in the work of the church and his sympathy had been expressed in a very practical form. Such sympsthy went a long way towards stimulating the humble efforts of each member of the congregation. At this stage Mr. Vaughan's little daughter Nora stepped forward and presented Mrs. Dodds with a hand- some bouquet. Continuing, Mr. VAITGHA.N said their existence as a church needed no justification. They had been estab- lished in Warrington about twenty-eight years, and they felt that God's blessing had rested upon the work from its commencement. The annual expenditure on the maintenance of the church, including the minister's stipend, was .2195. To that must be added a special effort which they had made during the year—the addi- tion of an organ which was very mudh needed, and which necessitated an outlay of about £125. Some two-thirds of that sum had already been paid. When he came to speak of the Institute he was faced with a still greater difficulty and heavier expenditure. The Institute might be said to have been an extension of their work, and it needed much faith to embark on such an enterprise. There was a debt of £1,400 still remain- ing on the building and property and to maintain the work of the Institute—which comprised Sunday school, temperance mission, social work amongst men and women, in fact almost every branch of Christian work they could crowd into the week—involved an annual expenditure of £7O, before they said any- thing about clearing off the debt or making grants to charitable and missionary enterprises. This, of course, represented a very great call on the resources of the congregation. With reference to the debt, he was pleased to say they had commenced to pay their way. They had already paid eft £25, for which they were profoundly grateful. To meet the great sum of about £2BO every year, they had neither pew rents, reserve fund, nor a single penny from any organization in con- nection with their church. All the money had to be raised by the voluntary offerings of their people Sunday by Sunday and the annual sale of work, when they received the help of outside friends. The continued progress of the church spoke a great deal for the devotion of their people, and the wonder was often expressed to him that the work had been enabled to succeed so far. He replied by saying that God's bless- ing rested upon them. They were trying to do His work in the way in which they believed He was leading them and,with the sympathy and prayerful help of their friends, they were hopeful of continuing on their way and increasing their fcrces. Last year's sale was a record, realizing £l5O, and he hoped this year's would be even more successful. (Applause.) Mr. Vaughan concluded by reading a list of donations, headed by £lO from Mrs. Dodds.

Mrs. DODDS, before declaring the sale open, thanked Mr. Vaughan for the kind things he had said about her. It was a great pleasure to come amongst them. There was a verse of a hymn which began "Let all who love the Lord join hands to aid the common cause" and she felt that she was coming to join hands with those who loved the Lord. When they realised how Warrington was being led and governed by the terrible drink, it was high time that Christian men and women, however they might differ as to form and ritual, joined hands and did their very utmost. She read in their Church " Messenger " how much prayer had been made and effort put forth in order that the sale might be a groat success. Could they doubt that it would be? 'They had there two of the greatest factors in the whole universe—prayer and work—and it was bound to be a success. She knew what a splendid set of workers they were and she deemed its great honour to come amongst them. She trusted the sale would be a record and had great pleasure in declaring it open. (Applanse.) On the motion of Mr. D. HoDGE, seconded by Mr. William ATHIERTON, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mrs. Dodds, and the sale commenced. The proceeds for the first day amounted to £63 Us. 2d., including 212 6s. in donations.

===== 0002601_19071116_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THURSDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

The sale was re-opened on Thursday, in the unavoid- able absence of the Rev. S. Pitt, by the Rev. THOMAS MILLER. of Bold-street Wesleyan Church. The Rev. F. VAITGIIA.N extended a very hearty welcome to Mr. Miller, and said it was gratifying to feel that they at Emmanuel Church had in a cause like that the sympathy of such a large circle of Christian people. They occupied a unique position among Chris- tian Churches, and they had every reason to be grateful both for the interest that had been generated in their own community all over the country and also for the kindly help and sympathy they received from other Free Churches. Emmanuel Church had always been remarkable for its active service and for the unity and harmony which had prevailed among its members. Such devotion to their cause was gratifying to him as their minister and was an evidence of the reality of the prin- ciples which they held dear as Protestant Churchmen. (Applause.) The Rev. THOMAS MILLER said that was the first time he had ever opened a sale of work or come into association with the Reformed Episcopal Church. He could assure them that it afforded him great pleasure to be present. They were all brethren of one Church; and the distinctions were very unimportant. Mentally they were of great value, and he would never sink any one of the principles in his mind. There was not the slightest need to do so. Their foe was a common one— the ignorance,sinfulness,drunkenness and gambling which abounded, and which were the enemy of all the Churches —and they needed all the unity in effort that they cduld muster to extend the Kingdom of God and reduce the power of evil, (Applause.) The Rev. J. F. MarrnEws moved a vote of thanks ,to the Rev. T. Miller, and expressed the hope that his I work at Bold-street would be so successful that he would pack his Church on Sundays just as the I school was packed for the popular concerts on Saturdays. Mr. J. BIBBY seconded, and trusted that Mr. Miller would often come amongst them during his stay in the Warrington circuit. The vote was cordially passed. • The total proceeds up to Thursday night amounted I to £lO2 13s. 3d. LAST EVENING'S PROCEEDINGS.

The sale was reopened last evening by the Rev. F. Vaughan who observed that they at Emmanuel were proving that it was possible to conduct spiritual work to sucaessful issues without the use of certain question- able methods. He trusted that in the future, as in the past. those friends who visited them and their chnrch, would find the atmosphere of their church and social life so full of the spirit of their unalterable principles that even their sales of work might be means of spiritual blessing to workers and visitors alike. He could not claim that their church was popular, in the general sense of the word, and probably it never would be, but he remembered much of God's most beautiful work was .performed quietly and away from the popular eye, and as he thought of their church, he ventured to think its methods of work, and its devoted service to man's deepest needs and God's glory, placed it among the first churches of the towns as a sphere where men and women might find such helpful and gracious ministries as to make it easier for them to live out their daily lives in the service of God.

Each day selections were given by Miss Thornton's orchestra and were much appreciated. The total proceeds of sale amounted to £l3l us. 3d.

"The Stooping Lady," by Maurice Hewlett

Macmillian and Co., Gs.

One usually takes up a novel by Maurice Hewlett, the author of" Forest Lovers," "The Queen's Qaair," etc., with a sense of pleasurable anticipation but in regard to his latest story "The Stooping Lady" we hardly think that the anticipation is realised. True enough the tale is original and written with a polish which denotes the practised and gifted hand, but it hardly does credit to the author's creative genius: We cannot help thinking that Mr. Hewlett might have given us something better and stronger. "The Stooping Lady" is not a book that will live," and we suppose it is every writer's desire to produce work of an undying description. The time of The Stoop- ing Lady's" advent is just a hundred years ago, when Cobbett was actively leading the radical reformers. In fact, Cobbett has a place in the book, and, hiatself pays homage to the heroine, Hermia Mary Chambre, who stoops to conquer for the rights of man. Hermia Mary is a strange heroine in many ways—a reader of Toni Paine, and the lover of a butcher. At the outset she conceives an admiration for a stalwart young butcher, clad in blue smack and bearing the implements of his trade, because she happens to see him chastise a couple of young bloods. The fact that one of these gentlemen turns out to be her cousin does not weaken the butcher's case, and when he is thrown into gaol and' kept there by the power of Family for some weeks, the subjugation of her heart is completed. Hermia Mary is not a silly impressionable creature )001,ny means, but a beautiful girl, the daughter of one of the highest families in the land and possessed of a noble nature and an unquenchable spirit. We could have forgiven her most things, but the incongruity of -her falling in love at first sight with the family butcher It is, of course, fortunate that the butcher, David Vernour, proves to be a young man dowered with I character and qualities which make him worthy of the high born Hermia Mary. His fight with Family , there is much about Family, with a capital F and , the powers that be, brings him into the political arena, where the devoted- Herima Mary resolutely follows him. Fate breaks the love-knot before it is properly tied, and saves the heroine from a hum- drum ending. The best feature of the story is the caricature of the Morfa family, one of the proudest in the land, and the most intolerant of the uplifting of the lower orders. With the Family Morfa's from the grim old Countess down- wards is everything. The Vernour incident is the first breach in the stronghold, which only just escapes Collapse through the treachery to tradition of Hermia Mary. An extract from the interview between Vernour's father and the Countess, daring the period of the butcher's imprisonment, presents a fine picture of the Toryism of the day. Vernour had pleaded for his son "A good lad, my lady, a clever, studious, spirited " Oh unlucky 'word! "Vernour, I can't have it, and I will not. Spirited! Do you know what was done? Do you know that his lordship was— touched? touched by your son—touched on the collar ?"That was as near to the fact as she could get herself to go."Do you know that Lord Edlogan was also touched—touched upon the—ah, person? Do you understand what these things mean? They mean Revolution, man; they mean the breakdown of the Constitution. They must be dealt with— and they have been dealt with. If persons act in this way, whole classes of persons may act so ; and society, and decency, and proper order must cease to exist. You are a sensible man, Vernour, and must surely perceive it." "Yes my lady, yes indeed," says broken old Vernour. "Very well. Then don't come to me talking of spirit, because that tells me that you hope to justify what cannot and must not be justified.' a a

"The Little Guest," by Mrs. Molesworth ; Macmillan and Co., 4s. 6d. We are pleased to announce the timely issue of one of Mrs. Molesworth's charming children's stories under the title of "The Little Guest." Christmastide without one of Mrs. Molesworth's bcnks would be a very dull period for many young people. No one can cater better for little minds and little hearts than the author of" The Ruby Ring," etc. Mrs. Molesworth's latest story is full of interest—and the interest is heightened by eight charming full-page illustrations by Gertrude Hammond. `,` The Little Guest" is a typical "present for a good girl," whose brothers will doubtless read it in turn with equal pleasure. " Arethusa," by F. Marion Crawford; Macmillan and Co.. 6s. Mr. F. Marion Crawford's new novel, " Aretbusa," is not of Rome. This is, in itself, a fact of interest, for Mr. Crawford has become identified with the Eternal City in the series of fine novels which he has penned about Rome and the Romans. His excursion this time to Constantinople provides a pleasant change from "A. Lady of Rome," "The Heart of Rome," " Cecilia," ond the Saraciuesca series. But wherever the scene, Mr. Crawford can be relied upon to weave an interesting romance, and we can honestly give warm praise to his creation of , " Arethusa.' The novel is not remarkable for depth, or even for the clever characterisation which is so noticeable a feature of the author's most famous fiction, but it is full of simple charm and ever-growing interest. Mr. Crawford's skill at word-painting is repeatedly manifested —indeed, we know of no one better able to 'conjure up in the reader's mind the picture which the printed pages are intended to pour- tray. Arethusa is a slave-maiden of noble birth, but shattered fortune, who comes into the possession of the hero, Carlo Zeno, a grand admiral of Venice who has greatly distinguished himself in war and diplomacy before he has reached the age of 30. The period of history is that in which the state of Venice and the Gencese are at bitter enmity one with the other. Though he has taken up his residence at the Golden Horn as a merchant Zeno soon becomes enveloped by the strong under-current of intrigue, and adventures suitable to his martial spirit follow as a natural course. Arethusa's advent is an early one,and this sweet girl, sold as a slave, captures the heart Of the hitherto unimpressionable Zeno. She plays a part in his fortures in the style of a true heroine, and finally gains the reward of beauty and virtue by becoming Carlo Zeno's bride. The story is very pleasantly told, and whether in the hands of readers young or old," Arethusa " will be found a source of quiet but real enjoyment.

MESSRS RXPUXEL TUCK & SONS CaIZIST'SAS

CARDS.

In making this season's announcement of their goods, Messrs. Raphael Tuck and Sons, Ltd., say : " We trust we may be able to finally make good our claim, and be voted that much coveted appointment of , Art Publishers to the Universe, by virtue of the unprecedented collection of Xmas. and New Year cards, calendars, books, engravings, picture post cards, toy books and art novelties put forth by us for the coming season in a lavishness of profusion, a perfec- tion of finish, and an originality of ideas which will be ' found to excel all our many successful efforts of previous years." It is said that " self praise is in recommendation," but the coatrary is the case here. Messrs. Tuck and Sons are entitled to all the praise which can be lavished upon them, for as art publishers and purveyors of the popular Christmas card they stand supreme. We have been permitted to see some ' of the novelties the firm has prepared for the coming season's trade, and we can only say that they appear to surpass, if pos3ible, all previous productions. There is apparently no "last word "in art publishing, and if there is it is not found in 'Messrs. Tuck's vocal) ilary. The Xmas and New Year Cards, for instance, are not only surpassingly beautiful and dainty, but many of them are quite originial in design. There are over 2,000 entirely new sets, including the " Art Etui " series from designs specially p-eparei for the King and Queen, and Prince and Princess of Wales, and now re-produced with their permission. There are the Vellum Duplex and Beaten Silver and Copper cards,which are also strikingly original and attractive. There is a particularly strong line also in boxed cards put up in transparent glace boxes, the sale for which should be . immense. Over 400 different calendars have been produced. Special among which are the art portfolios, and the sliding portfolios, the latter an ingenious and charming novelty. " Father Tuck " received a special appointment years ago to the nurseries of.the world, and he again proves worthy of the trust reposed in him with a huge assortment of lovely and interesting books. " Father Tuck's Annual "—that peerless production —is twice its old size at the same price, and chock full of the brightest reading and illustrations. Need we say anything about post-cards, we wonder? Tuck's are " the aristocrats of picture postcards " and for the Christmas season they have a vast new assortment which will delight the collector and the casual buyer alike. There is a series of state celebrities—real photographs from exclusive sittings—which should achieve special popularity, and altogether the range of Tuck's cards now comprises some 50,000 distinct designs.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

It is said of Sir Walter Scott that he could never tell another man's story exactly as he heard it, but must always be for giving it a cocked hat and a stick. Some of the opponents of Socialism have acquired a similar habit. I confess that lam not altogether sur- prised to find some Tories taunting Socialists with toadyism, and some Liberals libelling them with licentiousness. I wish that their obnoxious sibilations might cease, and that their sensorial organs might attract (and retain) something more weighty than purblindness. There are some who give quotations from books they have read • others from books they have not read. Hence confusion; and, occasionally, an apology. When one sees some of the many articles that are now oppearing in local and various other newspapers —to say nothing of the illustrated periodicals of the Limerick type —one wonders why, if Socialism is such a bad thing, so mnch energy should be expended in condemning it. There must be some other reason, surely, than the one we hear so much about just now. Do you know what it is? Is it, do you. think, that the people are becoming convinced that the most urgent needs of the State connot be provided for through the two great political parties, and that the time has arrived for the organizing of a new party? A.nd is not the Socialist party drawing to itself from each of the old parties those who are discontented with the progress made? The losses are disturbing to the Whips," and, thanks to their attitude, the success of the new movement has been considerably hastened. And so the new party is kept busy propagating the principles it stands for, and its opponents are equally hard at work in an endeavour to bluff the weak. The people are becoming more and more educated, and it is to the young men and women that the new party specially appeals.

IN REPLYING TO MR. C. a PARKER may I, first of all, offer him my hearty congratulations on his last letter. I may not altogether agree with the argument, but with the tone of it, I heartily concur. It is particularly pleasing to me to notice that he now ackaowledges that Christian Socialism and Modern Socialism spring from the same motive, viz. : a desire to help the condition of those who suffer under present unfair conditions. I remember, too, a few weeks , ago he stated that Maurice and Kingsley did not teach a single principle peculiar to modern Socialism bat opposed it with a virile force characteristic of the men. I have shown from the writings of Maurice, and have risked my reputation as the Editor of this column in giving numerous quotations to convince Mr. Parker, that the Christian Socialists did not oppose it with a virile force. Instead of opposing it, they welcomed it. Apd whilst they may have differed with Socialists, individually, yet with the principle —that of Co-opera- tion as against Competition—they were in full and hearty agreement. Those contentious matters being settled, we may now continue our way. I have argued that in an ideal State each shall give according to his ability and shall receive according to his needs. That, I consider to be the ultimate aim of Co-operation. It may be an ideal. I have yet to learn, however, that because we cannot suddenly attain an object that there is no good reason why we should strive after it and endeavour to get as near it as is possible. Christianity may be impossible to-day; yet I hesitate to imagine what would have happened if the people had refused to listen to, and in great measure to acknowledge, the teachings of Christ. Equality of sacrifice may be unattainable ; but that is no reason surely why we should advocate a system for human society grounded upon self-interest. Let n.e put it in another way. Free Trade as a policy is recognised as the best for people and nations but it is not yet possible. To suggest that the best way to hasten it is to ignore it altogether is almost as absurd as brewers of ale f profit advocating Temperance Reform by forcing their tied houses to sell more beer. Christ condemned riches. And yet there are many Christians to-day who will tell us that a system which makes it possi,ble for the rich to beconie richer should be bolstered no. To allow a man to heap up riches more than sufficient for his needs,and then to condemn him for so doing, is just as absurd, to my mind, as being paid for praying for the repose of the dead with the money that the deceased has sweated out of his employees. Some places money will not buy,and Heaven is one of them. There appear to me only two things for it : either we accept the principle of co-operation or we continue a policy of competition : which is it to be? Now Mr. Parker claims that each shall get the fruit of his labour, the whole fruit and nothing but the fruit. On the face of it, such a claim scans to be , reasonable.

The whole produce of labour is one of the Socialist systems that have been advocated and by some, notably in America, is still being advanced. What does such a system mean? We will agree, for the moment, that the right to the whole produce of labour is an economic right to the recognition of which the labourer has an unques- tioned claim. Each one gets the entire produce and nothing more. Labour alone is recognized. The strong would, doubtless, get more than the weak, and the infirm would not get anything. Unearned income, such as rent and interest, would be impossible. Persons working together would receive such a share as their contribution was worth. There are difficulties in the way, and I would like Mr. Parker to _say 1.19 w he suggests they would be overcome.

What Vrould hannen if there was no work for some of the lablurers? Would the State find them employment? What would happen if work intended to be pr.)duc- tive remained without result; for instance, if a farmer lost his crops? Would the State step in and support him during his temporary loss? I shall be pleased to know, before proceeding with my argument, whether he agrees with the points I hive raised as to the whole produce of labour. After that I shall try to shew him and " Perplexed " why the right to subsistence. I advocate should be recognized.

JOHN WORKNIA.N. THE PROGRESSIVk LA.BOUR LEAGUE.

I notice that the Warrington Progressive Labour League has been discussing Socialism; and I should be glad if you, Mr. Editor, or some of your correspon- dents, would be kind enough to inform me whether this party is, or is no', a branch of the Liberal party. If it is, why does it discard the good old name? If it is not, what then is its policy? Mr. Morton, speaking on the sth, said "he had not heard anyone yet who had defined Socialism in a way that it could not be questioned." Really! Is be quite sure that Liberalism can be defined in a way that cannot be questioned? Is this gentleman, or his party in favour of, or opposed to, Municipalisation or Nationalisation? If they are not entirely in favour of this policy how far are they prepared to go? I ask these questions in the hope of finding out 'exactly where we (Socialists) stand in regard to Liberalism, which is, I am afraid, as great a Ozzie to me as Socialism is to a number of my Liberal friends. Mr. Elwell, unlike Mr. Morton, does not appear to have any doubts as to what Socialism is. He says I" Socialism did not give an opportunity for advanced thought, but would say 'Now we have obtained per- fection, and now we are going to stop.'" This gentleman's foresight is really wonderful; but if we obtain perfection under Socialism, what more can we desire?

Mr. Elwell further says, "If there was going to be no capital how could a man bting his invention to perfection? ' A question like the above seems to me to be both ridiculous and absurd; because no Socialist of any standing ever said that there was going to be no capital. After Mr. Morton and Mr. Elwell, comes Mr. Howes. "Every agency in the country would be presided , over by a Government official, and there would thus be an enormous amount of officialism." Now Mr. Editor I contend that under Socialism there would be less officialism than there is to-day. Under the present system every office and every shop—no matter how small —is governed by an official of some sort ; whereas under Socialism a large number of the small offices and shops would be unnecessary. yo-day the small shop-keeper has either gone or is icang out of existence, and in his place has come, or is coming 1 the large limited companies, with shops in every town, and each shop managed by an official. Mr. Howes further says (under Socialism) that a man

who has a son may be required to go and do work in Birmingham and the son in Dindon and concludes from this that there would be no security for home life at all. This gentleman seems to forget that at the present time hundreds of boys have to leave home to find work and thousands of children as well as women have no home, because the system of com- petition has meant the death of the breadwinner. Before concluding might I remind Hr. Howes that the State under Socialism would mean the people. S. D.

" TffE WHOLE FRUITS OF HIS LA.BOUR."

Sir,—The letter written by Mr. Parker in your last week's issue is, in my opinion, one of the most valu- able contributions yet published in your very interest- ing column. I appreciate his gentlemanly and eloquent reply to my somewhat crude comments on his previous corres- pondence with "John Workman," but I must confess I still remain unconvinced that any substantial social benefits could possibly accrue from the general application of the principle that each man should receive "the whole fruits of his labour." Your correspondent apparently under-estimates the supreme and vital importance of securing (for this discussion) a clear, generally accepted definition of the term "the whole fruits of a man's labour." For instance when replying to my query as to the relative incomes that would be paid (under his suggested system) to clerics and scavengers, Mr. Parker answers: " What the community which requires these services is prepared to give in exchange." This explanatory sentence appears to me an illogical and contradictory deduction, and I would here point out to Mr. Parker that the community of Warrington. require the services of scavengers and street-sweepers, and are prepared to give in exchange for those services a weekly pittance of twenty-one shillings. In your last week's issue Mr. Parker stated: "If a man got the full reward of his labour, there would be sample margin to enable everyone to be independent in old age." Therefore according to this line of argument the Warrington scavengers and street-sweepers receive f "the whole fruits of their labour," 21 shillings per week. ! (that is the amount the community is prepared to give in exchange for their services) and have ample margin to enable them to be independent in old age. Does Mr. Parker really believe this inhuman fallacy? Not at all, say I, for in your last Saturday's column he boldly said: —" Flow can we expect a man earning twenty shillings a week to feed,clothe and house a family of five children in the standard of decency which our highly developed society demands, and at the same time save for declining years. Let any one who thinks it can be dnne, try it." The community have, for many years, been prepared to give large incomes to dukes, bishops, company promoters and Royal personages whilst they have allowed seamstresses, tailoresses, dock labourers and a large army of casual workers, to toil hard amidst unhealthy and disagreeable surroundings for starva- tion wages. On these (and similar) grounds, Sir, I contend that Mr. Parker's explanation of the term "the whole fruits of a man's labour" is apparently illogical and even contradictory. After writing the above I carefully read three times his letter of Saturday last, and in spite of his pains- taking reply to my initial contribution, unfortunately, on this extremely important subject of social reform I still remain more or less

Howley Ward Election.

Sir,—My attention has been called to a statement contained in a letter of thanks issued to the electors by Councillor W. B. Roberts. Alluding to the fact that. he did not offer any public thanks or address after the declaration of the poll he says " my sole reason for not doing so was that a suggestion was made by Mr. Clegg. and his party that no speeches were to be made out- side, which arrangement I honourably carried out." I take the opportunity offered by your columns to say that no such suggestion or arrangement was ever men- tioned in my hearing,nor was thera so far as I have been able to discover any word to that effect spoken to any of my supporters. Had a proposal to that effect been made made I should certainly have declined to allow anyone to-. deter me from doing my first duty to the electors by. publicly tendering my thanks.—l am,yours truly,

WILLIAM CL'EGG.

WARRINGTON FOOTBALL CLUB. The Proposed Benefit to D. Isherwood:,

A crowded spscial meeting of the members of the Warrington Foctball Club was held last night on the requisition of -certain members to consider the question of giving a benefit match to D. Isherwood, the popular threequarter. Mr. J. E. Warren, president of the Club presided: Mr. H. ROGEM, the convenor of the meeting, pro- posed that Isherwood ha given a benefit match fOr his valuable services tq the Club and mentioned the names of some half-dozen clubs which had given benefits to their players. Mr. H OrtosßY seconded and a lot of discussion followed.

Mr. Asarco: read a report which he had presented to the Committee. The Committee thought the matter should be left to their judgment. H. Forster was the oldest playing member af the Club, having com- menced in 1891. Isherwood started in 18.95. They thought a benefit match as suggested would be an awkward precedent. Mr. NORHIN proposed that the Club start a testi- monial instead of a benefit match, awl the amend- ment was seconded by Mr. Habberley. On a show of hands the amendment was lost.

A. ballot was taken for or against a benefit match, and the affirmative was carried by 278. votes against 56. It was decided unanimously that all members pay for aduaission to the benefit match. Details, the fixing of the match, etc., were left in the hands of the Committee. It was decided that the expenses of the match be deducted from the receipts.

MOTOR CAB 4 FOR BIRAINGHAM, Following London's example, Birmingham is to be provided with motor cabs. The West Aster, Ltd., are placing on the streets a hundred beautifully equipped vehicles, making the pleasure of riding in an up-to-date cab available to all who can spare the shilling or two for the enjoyment of this—one of the most fascinating war of getting about town or country. The cabs are varied, some open and luxuriously fitted for two persons, others for four; some will be fitted with powerful engines and provided with means of carrying baggage, as well as four passengers. These new machines will be of the taximeter kind. We are informed that the West Aster Ltd. are-about to exploit the chief towns of the Midlands with a similar, service, and although the introduction of motors will, no doubt, displace a good many horse cabs, the Company offer to take the drivers and train as chaffeurs. Birmingham is to be congratulated on this innovation.

PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.---Advt. Thomas Morris Charity. , Saturday last being the day appointed for the dis- tribution of this charity by the late Hr. Thomas Morris, Mr. Arthur Browne, solicitor, the honorary corres- ponding trustee of the charity, distributed a pair of blankets each to 65 old women, and a red flannel petticoat to 50 old women, all of Warrington. The petticoats were supplied by Mrs. Joseph Harrison, of the Charity Organization Society, and the blankets byl Messrs. Pattison and Perkins, of Sankey-street, and Mr. Clegg, of Church-street, who were selected by the trustees. Lectures on " Plant Life." The fourth of the series of University Extension lectures on "Plant Life" was delivered in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Wednesday even- ing by Professor F. E. Weiss, D. Sc. There was again a good attendance. The lecturer dealt with the common cone-hearing trees and their distinctive characters, catkin-bearing trees, and the trees with blossom. He also touched upon the fertilisation of flowers and "plants and their unbidden guest." The lecture was most interesting and instructive and was listened to with great attention. The next lecture of the course will be delivered on November 27th. ' TABLE, PLOoR AND HANGING LAMPS. New Designs now showing.—J, S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge- strPet, Warrington.—Advt. • Warrington Musical Festival. For several years efforts have been made to encourage local musical talent hy holding an annual Warrington Eisteddfod. The enterprise has been undertaken by the Apollo Prize Male Choir and has met with a considerable measure of success. It is felt, however, that the time has arrived when War- rington should come into line with many other towns and have a musical festival promoted by as large and representative a circle of musical enthusiasts as possible The project has now been placed on a definite footing and the result is that the Eisteddfod will give place to the Warrington Musical Festival which will be held in the Parr Hall towards the end of March next. A strong committee has been formed and a well-attended meeting to appoint officers and make preliminary arrangements was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe on Saturday evening. Great enthusiasm and confidence were manifested and everything augurs for a highly successful festival. Mr. E. Lloyd-Jones was elected chairman of the com- mittee; Mr. N. Cook, vice-chairman; Mr. Frank Hickman, treasurer; Mr. R. W. Cook, general secre- tary. An executive committee was appointed to draw up a syllabus. We hope the festival will receive that full measuse of support from lovers of music which it certainly deserves, and that the success achieved will justify the continuance of the event annually. P.S A.

An open meeting of the Wycliffe Society was held on Sunday when there was a good attendance. Councillor Ball presided, and spoke upon "Taking sides" or, "A Christian life and true citizenship.' Speaking to a mixed gathering he said there was a great future for women in taking their share in the municipal life of the towns. Men worked in public life from reason and intellect. Wisely so, but women brought their intellect to bear with the depth of their heart feelings, which was very helpful in public as well as social life. Mr. Frank Lord, of Manchester, gave a stirring address, whiili included some experi- ences of his sinful life as a burglar, brought about by sinful environments. He was glad to say what Goa had done for him He could do for the lowest and most rel4ellions. (Applause.) Miss A. Griffiths sang "0 Divine Redeemer," and Mr. T. Brown "The Radiant Morn," and both were greatly appreciated. Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ. The Rev. J. Yonge and Mr. Colley also took part in the meeting. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHTRE.—Advt. Can a Man be a Good Citizen P This question was discussed in a paper read at a meeting of the Literary and Philosophical Society on Monday evening by Mr. H. N. Fairbrother, M.A., of Linc-,ln College, Oxford. Mr. A. J. Jolley presided over a good attendance. Mr. Fairbrother said the question, simple as it seemed, was one to which history bad seldom been able to give a satisfactory reply. In every department of common life—social, commercial, political -a divergency between private and public morality forced • itself upon us. The minister at Westminster reflected sadly "how hard it is for a statesman to do right," and the minister's wife in Kensington had a troubled conscience aver the necessity of Society lies which were none the less shocking for being called "white." Quakers and anti-vaccinators found it hard to reconcile their law of right with the law of the State. As a com- munity was, theoretically, one, they must work at all times towards organization, not disorganization. When, a few months ago, the leader of the Labour Party in Parliament declared that what he had "tried to do was to make his own class the ruling class," and added that he" believed this idea was on the way to fruition" he gave expression to a spirit and a hope which animated, he trusted, very few of their leading men. The message they needed their Statesmen to deliver was one of true patriotiQm, not of party spite, and its spirit should be that of President Roosevelt or Lord Cromer, not that of Mr. Keir Hardie. With regard to any definite difference between themselves and a political opponent, let them adopt the homely rule " Put yourself in his place ";• in other words try to estimate their opponent's view from his stand- point. It was at least possible they might find much' common ground underlying the external divergence of expression ; and that the difference which they too hastily regarded ns a difference of principle might, after all, lie only in the form of presentation. A spirit of compromise would take them many steps lin the right direction, but the compromise must be I real, not sham. They must be specially on their guard against shiblyleths and watch-cries. An interesting discussion followed, in which the President, Mr J. Rymer Young, Mr. Rossiter, Mr. Lofthouse, Mr. S. Kenyon, and Mr. E. J. Hall took part, a hearty vote of thanks being accorded to Mr. Fairbrother, on the motion of Mr. Young, seconded by Mr. Rossiter. DOULTON TOILET WARFI and Dinner Sets. See 1 Windows.—J. S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Stretton Publican Fined At Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, 'before Mr. Williaii Long (in the chair) and Mr. H. Nield, William _ - -- - - - - -

Long Greaves, landlord Of the Ring.o'. Bells Inn,

Lower Stretton,was summoned for perm- itting drunken- ness on his prmises at 9.10 p.m. on October 26th, and James Povey, labourer, Lower Stretton, was summoned for being drank on licensed premises. Mr. Steel, who apaea red to defend, pleaded not guilty. Mr. W. H. Churton,of Chester, who prosecuted,said Acting- Sergeant Kennerley called at the house at 9.10 p.m. on October 26th, and in the tap-room found eight persons. Among them va-I the defendant Povey and Kennerley saw that he was drunk. He spoke to Povey, who, with con4iderable difficulty, got on to his feet ; in fact he had to take hold of a table to support him- self. Povey then took a drink out of a glass in front of him. The officer at once called the attention of the licensee to the man, and told him he wae drunk. The

licensee did-not attemrt to say one word to the contrary and during the whole time the officer was in the house

it was neve—r contradicted that the defendant was drunk. Mrs. JAnson, a daughter of Povey, appeared on the scene, and said, "That is right, _Mr.. Kennerley, it is

time something was done. He is drunk ana they keep bringing tim beer. He has had four gills in twenty minutes." He (Mr. Churton) supposed that was too express a speed.— The defence was that Povey was not drunk and it was stated that he only had five glasses of beer in the house. A large number of witnesses gave evidence on behalf of the defendant who has held the liewee of the Ring-o'-Bells for 24 years.—The Bench retired to consider the case, and on returning the Chairman said Greaves would be fined and costs. They were sorry after be had held the licence so long to have to convict, but it was their duty odo so. They, hoped tile owners of tbe house would take into consideration defendant's past character in their treatment of the

house in the.future. They allowed £2 2s. advocate's fee. The defendant Povey would be fined 2s. 6d. and costs, and they hoped this would be a lesson to him. It was evident that the house had been too much of a

resort on Saturday nights, and if the 7 could the Bench would break such habits. Drinking four or five glasses of beer in twenty minutes was not taking advantage of a meeting with neighbours, and they were all wishful to stop that as far as they could.

Warrington Bandmaster's Death. The announcement will be received with regret in local brass band circles of the sudden death of Mr.l Patrick James Brady, who had been bandmaster of Walker's Brewery Band from its formation. The deceased, who was 38 years of age and resided at 83, Liverpool-road, Warrington, had been missing from home since Wednesday of last week. On Sunday night he was found unconscious on a seat on the Liverpool Landing Stage. He was at once removed to the David Lewis Northern Hospital where he died. Mr. Brady was much esteemed by his fellow members of the Brewery Band, having taken great pains to secure its progress and efficiency. The funeral will take place at the Warrington Cemetery this (Saturday) afternoon. New Century Pictures at Parr Hall. , Mr. Sydney Carter has this week continued his pro- gramme of highly amusing and instructive pictures at the Parr Hall. There has been a large and apprecia- tive audience each evening and the programme -- a complete change from last week's—has given general pleasure. The most interesting picture is that of a tour through the slate quarries in Port Dinorwic, Wales. The audience is enabled in imagination to inspect the whole of the quarry which is the largest of its type in the world. Another fine view depicts His Majesty's visit to the "Dreadnought" -the huge vessel which has recently been added to the British Navy. A holiday in lovely Switzerland is splendidly illus- trated and to audience is able to form a good idea of the grandeur of the mountain and lake scenery. A host of humorous pictures create uproarious mirth. The entertainment throughout is well in keeping with the New Century Company's high reputation, and those of our readers who have not yet witnessed it should make a point of doing so. There will be a special show for the children this (Saturday) after- noon, and the final performance will be given this evening. Volunteer Manceuvres. The C and D Companies of the Warrington Volun- teers carried out some interesting manoeuvres in the Grappenhall neighbourhood on Saturday, the tigheme having been drawn up by Lieutenant-Colonel Eddlsten. The men turned out in very fine numbers, and were accompanied by the Regimental Band. The Company officers provided a meat tea at 5.30 in the Grappenhall Parish Room, kindly lent by the Rector of Grappen- hall. After tea a smoking concert was held, when songs were contributed by Colour-Sergeant Taylor, ex-Pioneer Sergeant Broadhurst, Private Glover, Private Tickle, Bcc. A "feu de joie," was then fired in the Rectory garden, accompanied by the playing of the National Anthem, in honour of the King's birth- day. After the "Last Post" was sounded by the Buglers, the band played an evening hymn, and the two Companies then marched back to the Drill Hall and were dismissed. Mr. Charlton Parr kindly put his park at the disposal of the officers, and, owing to this privilege, all concerned were enabled to have an interesting and instructive afternoon. The following officers were on parade :—Captain G. R. Mott (adjutant), Major F. M. Appleton (commanding Red), Captain Owen (commanding Blue), Lieutenant Monks, Lieutenant Woods, and Sergeant-Major Ingram. PUBLIC BENEFIT—UNDRR, THR CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. A Critic of Garden Cities. The members of the Warrington Branch of the Amalgamated Union of Operative Bakers and Confec- tioners held their annual tea at the Lion Hotel on Saturday evening. Mr. W. H. Carr presided over a large gathering and amongst others present were : Councillors Steel, Evans and Stott, and Messrs. W. Creighton, W. Millington, J. Berry, W. Gregson, J. Greeley, W. Rathbone (district secretary), and H Hickinbottom (secretary of the Warrington branch. Councillor Steel, responding to the toast of "The Town and .Trade of Warrington,"said,they had heard a great deal recently about garden cities, but he could not see what good a garden city out at Penketh was going to be to the working classes of Warrington. (Hear, hear.) A working man must be close to his work. If he lived at Penketh, his children had to be educated, and a working man could not afford to pay car or railway fares every day. In his opinion what they ought to do was to see that the dwellings of the working men in the town were improved. (Applause.) He hoped that instead of having garden cities outside the town, there would be some consideration given to houses to be built in the town. (Applause.)—An enjoyable concert was gone through, songs being given by Miss P. Taylor, and Messrs. J. E. Hannan, W. G-regson, T. W. Davies, C. Knowles, and W. Shelley, and a recitation by Mr. W. H. Carr. Mr. J. Isherwood sang several humorous Kings. In Pursuit of Game. At Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, before Mr. William Long (in the chair), and Mr. H. Nield, three Warrington men named William Warburton, junior, 72, Bostock-street •' Peter Rowson, junior, 26, Bowes-street; and Henry Faulkner, Plumpton street, were summoned for entering upon land in the occupa- tion of Henry Dodd at Lower Walton in pursuit of game. Faulkner did not appear. Mr. Steel repre- sented the defendants. -Mr. E. Draper for the prose- cution, stated that at 11.30 a.m. on October 20th three men were seen to cross the river Mersey in a boat from near Monks, Hall and Company's Works and to land on property owned by Sir Gilbert Greanall. A short time afterwards John Earlam, a keeper employed by Sir Gilbert, heard two shots fired—what he called the "a double shot "—and this attracted him to the spot. Immediately after the shots were made a covey of partridges flew from the place over his head. Earlam then saw three men go out of the field into another fiell nearer the river. He went as fast as he could toward the men, and heard other shots fired by one of them.- • Mr. Steel said Warburton had been in the employ of Monks, Hall and c.30.,f0r about two years,and for twelve years prior to that he was with Crosfield and Sons and was a man of very good character. It was a fact thac Warburton's father had a boat on the river, lying there for the winter, and on the Sunday morning in question he crossed the river to the boat. As to what happened afterwards, he (Mr, Steel) was not going to contest the evidence of the prosecution, and he hoped that this would be a lesson to Warburton. —After a short retirement, the Chairman said the Bench had not much difficulty in coming to a decision. Rowson and Faulkner, who were well known to the police, would be fined 30s. and costs each or two months' imprisonment in default, and Warburton ss. and costs. They hoped this would be a lesson to Warburton to abstain from this sort of things.—Mr. Draper applied for advleate's fee, and the Chairman said the Bench had awarded 10s. 6d. against each of the defendants. —John Russell, farm labourer, Stretton, was fined ss. and costs for being on land in the occupation of Alfred Faulkner, Moss Hall Farm, Stretton, in pursuit of game. Bravery Rewarded. Bravery was fittingly rewarded at the -Borough Police Court on Monday, when the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) presented three parchment , certificates, given hy the Royal Humane Society, and framed by himself, to Thomas G-roak, aged 15, Oxford-street; John Lucy, labourer, Bishop's Wharf; and John Starkey, labourer, John-street, for their gallantry and humanity in saving life at Bridge Foot. Just after four o'clock on Saturday, afternoon, August 23th, Arthur Woods, aged six years., son of Thomas Woods, of 78, Wellington-street, was playing on the Packet Stage at Bridge Foot, when he fell into the river, and was in imminent danger of drowning, when Thomas Groak very gallantly indeed, although quite unable , to swim, entered the waterivithout a moment's hesita- tion or thought for his owli safety, waded in until he he was about to be swept off his feet, and made a very plucky effort to reach Woods. Meantime John Lucy, a workman at Bishop's Wharf, heard the alarm given, saw Woods being carried into deep water, and at once entered the river fully clad, and swam to the child's assistance. The boy Groak was by this Cone almost out of his death and in great difficulty and danger, when another workman John Starksy, came on the scene, took in position of affairs at a glance, eutered the water and first assisted Groak by pushing him to the Ade, where he was seized by Constable Roberts, and then assisted Lucy, in rescuing Woods. By Lucy's efforts, assisted by Starkey, Woods was brought to the side and lifted out I after which "First aid" was promptly rendered to I him by Constable Robe! ts. The pluck, judgment, and resource displayed by all the recipients of the parch- ments reflected the greatest credit upon them. Mr. F. Crabtree, a tradesman carrying on business at Bridge Foot, who was on the scene almost immediately the rescue was effected, appealed by letter to the Chief. Constable with a view to the action of the parties concerned being brought under the notice of the Royal Humane Society. The Mayor handed the certificates I to the rescuers, amidst great applause. Addressing I Mr. Crabtree, he said it was a credit to him to have. taken such an interest in the case, and to have written such a beautiful letter, which had been the means of obtaining these three people ceotificates. The Bench and the town were much obliged to him. He wished to express his thanks to the policeman for rendering first aid. He also thanked the ambulance corps for the magnificent and useful work they had done during the past. (Applause.)

Christian Endeavour Convention. Visit of the President of the British Christian Endeavour Union.

Considerable interest was taken In the autumnal con- vention of the Warrington and District Union of Young Peoflle's Societies of Christian Endeavour which was held on Saturday, WI the members were favoured with a visit from the President of the British Christian Endeavour Union (Mr. R. W.Davies,J.P.dof Darlington. In the afternoon a meeting was held in Brick-street mission when an exercise "-Shining for Jesus" was given by junior Endeavourers from Emmanuel, Bank Quay, Brick-street, Friars Green and Legh-street, under the leadership of Miss Gandy. Miss M. Allen gave the connective readings. The children acquitted themselves very creditabl y and the exercise proved most interesting. rhe Rev. F. Vaughan (president of the Union) presided and in a brief address emphasised the good influences of child life which, he said, should be treasured as helps in Christian work. After the meeting, tea was served by the ladies of the Brick-street congregation. Towards the close Mr. Davies, President of the British Christian Endeavour Union,arrived and a brief conference on union work was held. Mr. Davies gave sound advice as to successful methods of propagating Christian endeavour in both the young people's and the junior sections. In the evening a meeting was held in St. John's Presby- terian Church. The Rev. F. Vaughan presided and said that was the first time in the history of Christian Endeavour work in that neighbourhood that they had had the pleasure of a visit from the President of the British Union—Mr. Davies gave a simple yet powerful and touching address on Christian Endeavour work. He said he had been present at very many Christian Endeavour meetings during the past few weeks. He had had the opportunity of meeting a great many Endeavourers and he had come to regard it as an increasing pri vi lege because he was always able to derive some useful hints from the conferences. He wanted to stir up the interest of Endeavourers and he would tell them several stories to illustrate different phases of the work undertaken by societies. They existed not merely for the sake of the great advantage of the weekly meet- ing but for the purpose of.helping young people to begin to serve Christ and of stimulating them in that service. He was old fashioned enough to believe in open air preaching of the gospel, in tract distribution from •door to door, and were not Christian Endeavourers the right people to do that? God used the young to win the young, and it was the highest work in which they could engage. Mr. Davies then narrated anecdotes showing how Christian Endeavourers could be useful in many ways.—An interesting feature of the meeting was Mr. bavies' questioning of the representatives of the various societies as to how they conducted their work. He strongly emphasised the necessity of each society receiving monthly reports from its commitees.He desired Christian Endeavourers to put the same energy into their Christian Endeavour work as they would into their own businesses. Mr. Davies also answered questions asked by the Endeavourers as to the difficulties which they experienced. —Mr. J. Willett gave a feeling inter- pretation of the solo " The Man of Sorrows."

MY DEA I am glad to say that I feel mor to write you my usual letter to-day. I have sent several mirrors away this week to my members, as my post-bag has contained such a large amount of stories and anecdotes. Now November is getting on, and I do want you to do your best before Christmas to send me something for the, poor. Remember how some children must suffer without any scarves or gloves during the cold weather that is coming. These little gifts do not cost much, although they take up a little time, which I am sure you will not grudge. To the senders of the best articles I shall award boxes of chocolates at Christmas-time. I have had several nice personal letters, and welcome some new members. I am pleased to receive the names of Edith and Evelyn Croker. Much sympathy to Elsie Talbot. I trust you will soon be well again; tell your sister she shall have a mirror.

Now, good-bye till next week, when I will tell you about some Christmas competitions. UNCLE NED.

THE HARE AND THE TORTOISE. One day a hare met is. tortoise. He said, "What a lazy fellow you are; you cannot get along very well." How do you make that butt" said the tortoise. "I can beat you in a race!" " Come on," said the hare; "which shall be the goal-post?" "The old pine-tree," said the tortoise. "Agreed!" cried the hare. Then they started. The hare darted off, and the little tortoise came plodding along. The hare ate some clover, and found it very good. After eating a lot, he went to sleep, and the tortoise reached the goal-post first. All of a sudden the hare awoke, and remembered the race. Away he darted, but it was no good—he found the tortoise first. SYDNEY TALBOT.

A farmer once had a gentleman visitor, and one day the visitor asked the farmer for some- thing to do, so the farmer told him to milk his cow, and he gave him the stool and the can. In about half-an-hour he came in again; he was all red and hot, so he told him he hact been trying to make the cow sit. on the stool, and of course he could not do it. F. HAYWARD.

A kind gentleman was one day visiting some of the poorest ,parts of London. Leading from one garret was a ladder. "Surely," thought he, "there is no one up there; anyhow, I will go and see." Up the stairs he went, and, knocking at the door, he said, "Is there any one in here?" "Come in," said a feeble voice. He went in, and saw a poor little boy, lying on a bed of straw. "Are you alone?" inquired the man. "Yes," he replied. "My father is a drunkard. He used to send me ,out to steal, but I went to a ragged school, and 'learnt how to love God, and) I won't steal now, so he steals himself, and drinks the money." "Poor little boy," he said. "Don't you feel lonely all by yourself?" No," the boy answered. "Jesus is with me." "Well, I will see what I can do," said he. "Here i 9 some money to buy some food." After thank- ing him for the money, the little boy bade farewell to the gentleman. The next day he brought a lady to see him, and brought him some clothes, but when he went in he found the boy dead. ELSIE TALBOT.

BORN IN HIS OWN CHURCH.

Canon William Hector Lyon, known as the Blind Vicar of Sherborne, who is lying ill, has on medical advice resigned the living after forty years' ministry. He was actually born in his own church, his parents onctuping Abbey Lady Chapel ar a dwelling house. He is in his 81st year.

THE FOUR MONKEYS.

A ship was once sailing from the West Indies to England. Just when the anchor was about to be heaved up, the captain took on board four monkeys which were at times per. to wander aboiiit the ship at will. He had also procured a quantity of very fine grapes. These were hung up in his cabin; but in some unaccountable way the bunches disappeared much more quickly than they ought to have done. As monkeys have ever been a pilfering race, the sly group on board were at once sus- pected of unlawfully possessing themselves of the fruit. A watch was set for them, but for some time in vain. The captain, therefore, determined to keep watch himself, and, that he might mete out due punishment to the offenders when caught, he provided himself with a rope's end. One morning, just before the monkeys were let out of their cage, he lay in his cabin and pretended to be asleep. No sooner was he settled than, bounce! down came the whole party from the deck. They halted, however, at the door as if surprised to find the cabin. occupied. At length one, more bold than the rest, advanced, and, mounting the table ivarily approached the motionless captain. *After some time he lifted his paw, and gently raised the captain's eyelid to see if he really were asleep, the others watching the result of the experiment. The whole thing so amused the captain that he loudly laughed, whereupon the whole tribe scam- pered back to the deck. ELSIE TALBOT.

Dear 'Uncle Ned,—l should very much like to join the "Children's Corner," if I may. I enjoy reading it very much. We have not many pets, but we have two eats; one is nearly nine years old. We have several chickens, and one is mine. My sister Evelyn, who is nine years old, also wishes to join the "Corner." I will write again soon if we are accepted as members. EDITH CHOKER.

The daughter of a City magnate met at a City banquet not long ago a certain noble marquis whom she had had the pleasure of being intro- duced to during her father's term of Lord Mayoralty. She bowed to the noble marquis, who immediately recognised her, and as she sat next him at dinnlr they entered into conversa- tion. After a few preliminaries the young lady startled the noble marquis by the following abrupt query: "Do you think I get pretty, my lord?'" The noble marquis was a little taken aback ttt such a pointed question; however, he disguised his astonishment and answered gal- lantly, "Do I think you get pretty, Miss —I No, you are, and always have been, pretty—it is not a question of getting." What further' compliments the too polite marquis might have Indulged in it is impossible to conjecture, had he not been checked by the lady, who, with a face suffused with modest blushes, said: "I —I think you must have misunderstood me, my lord; meant, do you think 'lghgate pretty?" Then it dawned upon the unhappy nobleman that his fair questioner had been asking his opinion of that charming suburb which, in West End circles, is more frequently known as Highgate.

A FAIRY TALE.

There was once a poor woman, who had threes sons. Her husband had gone out into the world to seek his fortune.. She had heard nothing of him since he left them, so they were very anxious about his safety. Now it happened one day that the three brothers made up their minds to go and seek their father and their fortune. They went and told their mother their intentions, and, after much persuasion, she consented to let them go. Well, they started off with but one day's pro- visions, for their mother could not spare any more. When night came on they rested under a tree, and were soon fast asleep. In the midst of their sleep a fairy came flying past, and saw them sleeping. She said to herself, "I will give these boys a wish each, but I will wish it for each. The eldest shall have a sword that will cut anything; the second, a horn that will bring food; and the youngest., a purse that will bring money." When the boys awoke they were delighted with their presents, and, when the eldest was trying his sword on the trunk of a neighbouring oak tree, to his surprise the tree fell. Then the second was trying his horn, and, lo and behold! the tree stump became covered with savoury food. They went on after they had eaten, and they came to the foot of a hill. Here the third brother opened his purse, but found it empty. He iaid, "I wish it was full." No sooner had he said this than it be- came full of gold pieces. They then sat down on the grass and ate a hearty meal. Again they started their travels uphill.. When they got to the top they saw a large castle. On one side of it was a small window, but instead of glass there were iron bars, looking through which was their father. Oh, how they jumped' for joy! The eldest son cut the bars, and his father got through. The second so it gave him a meal, and then they all four went home, V' the delight of their mother.

THE TWO BEARS.

Once there were two people, who 41 not been married very long. Once, when the husband came home from walking, he said, "I have got ,to let two bears live with us. They have got to sleep with us and eat with us.' "Two bears 1"' said his wife. "Two, bears," replied the husband. • "They can sleep in the brewhouse, but they will not come in my kitchen," the wife said. "But where I go these bears must go,'• replied the husband. "I shall leave you, then," the wife said. "No," he said; the two bears are ' to- bear ' and 'forbear.'" DORIS TALBOT.

MY PET DOG.

My pet is a dog. It will follow me about wherever I go. Its name is Carlo. Carle is a very nice dog. The colour of him is black. He comes and lies inside the door, and if I am alone; and any one comes to the door, he will begin to growl. Carlo often has games with me. One game we play is "hide-and- seek." If he cannot find me, he begins to whine, and when he finds me he jumps up and down, as much as to say, "I've found you." I have had Carlo three years come Christmas. I had him given to me for a Christmas-box. I love Carlo. and I believe Carlo loves me. He will not let any one touch me. W. ELLIOTT.

OUR PET CAT.

One of our favourite pets is a black and white cat. He has a black tail, two black spots on Eats head, and two on his back. He is a most faithful cat, and also loving. When all are still, he will jump on your lap,and put his paws round your neck in a most loving way. He is more of a show cat than a mouse- hunter. My father has a lot of pigeons: he keeps them in a nice, whitewashed pen. Tom. the cat, who is very fond of pigeons, stole one. We were just in time to get it off him, but V . . was dead. D. TALBOT.

"What is your business ? " said the judge at the police-court the other morning to a prisoner. "I'm an observationist, your Honour." "An observationist I What is that ? " "One who looks round in the daytime to see what he can steal at nignt, if it pleases your Honour." It did not please his Honour, and so he sent the observationist to jail for two months.

A rule of the railway companies provides that dogs shall not ride in passenger carriages, but a big and ferocious-looking bull-dog walked into a carriage the other day, appropriated a whole seat, and rode two hundred miles unmolested. "He had such a meaning smile," was the guard's apology for not ejecting him.

PILES FOR 17 YEARS.

Five Operations Fail Final Cara by

Zam-Buk.

After five operations bad failed to cure Mr. T. G. P. Boniface, of 38, Kinsman-street, of piles and complications, Zam-Buk brought him miraculous relief and has now given him sound health. Mr. Boniface, who is a well-known member of the Company of Watermen and Lightermen, said to a W oolw ich Herald " reporter :

"I suffered from piles for seventeen years, and was in almost continuous agony for weeks Cogether. Two local doctorsadvised an operation, and I tried various so-called remedies for piles-- ointments, lotions, and medicines—which did not do the least good. The pain became so intense that I entered a Seaman's Hospital and submitted to an operation, remaining in the hospital nearly a month. They did all that was possible at the time, but shortly afterwards I was again in such pain that I went to a local Cottage Hospital, where I was operated upon and treated for ,our weeks. Still I was not cured, and I entered another ' hospital which devotes itself entirely to such cases, and was twice operated upon for prolapse and rectal abcess. I was in the hospital seven weeks. The surgeon who performed the second operation told me that owing to the unavoidable results of so many operations I should probably have to come up again. Although not free from pain I managed to go on for four years, when the surgeon's prediction was fulfilled, and after a fifth operation I remained in hospital nine days. "I can scarcely describe the anguish I suffered. For weeks at nights when I ought to have been asleep, I walked about in the 'nest intense pain. " Last year I was thinking seriously about another operation when one of my boys happened to fall and cut his forehead severely. I sent for -some Zam-Buk and applied it to the wound, which healed perfectly in four or five, dap:. I du.. triod Zam-Bak myself for the piles, applying the balm according to directions. By the time I had used •one box I was relieved of all pain. I continued the treatment whenever I thought there was the least sign of a return of the trouble, and now for ,months I have felt as well as when I was a young man. I consider myself properly and permanently 'cured entirely by Zam-Buk, which I have recom- mended to dozens of people suffering from piles."

TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

. The monthly meeting of this Council was held in the I.L.P. TOollls on Tuesday evening, the newly elected president Mr. W.H.ltiobardson in the chair. There was a good attendance. The CHAIRMAN in opening the proceedings stated that it was his first occasion to take the ohair of the Trades and Labour Council that he would try and do his utmost to see that everything is carried on in a straightforward and honest manner. He hoped the members of the Council would support Mm. (Applause.)

AUDITORS REPORT.

•Councillor STOTT stated that the presentation of the balance sheet was adjourned until that meeting. Mr. Hickinbottom and himself had attended along with the treasurer and secretary, and had gone through the accounts which they they had found correct. They had a balance in hand of a 14s. 4id. upon which they could be congratulated. (Hear, hear.) The receipts amounted to £3l 145...9d. and the total expenditure to £2B Is. 4id. ; The balance sheet was adopted.

LANCASHIRE CONGREGATIONALIST'S DEATH.

The death took place oil Sunday, at his residence at Hendon, of the Rev. Henry W. Smith, a Congregational minister, widely known throughout Lancashire. Mr. Smith was trained for the Congregational ministry at Hackney College, and became minister of Stoney Stratford Church in 1872, remaining there five years. In 1877 he removed to Fleetwood, and afterwards became the pastor of Centenary Church, Lancaster, where he laboured for twenty-one years. Throughout the county he was highly respected and esteemed, and on his retirement from the active ministry, two years ago, and after a residence for a time in Switzerland, he went to reside at Hendon. in 1902 Mr.Stnith was chair- man of tha Lancashire Congregational Union, which celebrated its centenary this year.

George Stopford, a platelayer on the Great Central Railway, while at work near glsecar Junction on Tues- day, was knocked down by a train. Four wagons passed over his legs. A Corporation labourer, Frederick Jackson, of Long- street, Great Ancoats, employed at the Water-street health yard, bad three fingers and a thumb taken off by a rolling machine on Tuesday.

IS LIFE WORTH LIVING?

It all depends on the state of health. If the liver is not properly carrying out its functions the appetite becomes small and fanciful, the skin sallow, the eyes dull, and in the morning the answer to the above query will be "NO." Care should be taken to impress upon the chemist the necessity of dispensing this medicine exactly as prescribed.
