The Board resolved :--" That inasmuch as Dr. Richard Sephton has, for many yesrs past, satisfactorily filled the office of Medical Officer for No. 3 district of the Union, and his president residence and surgery, although situated out of the district, is accessible end Convenient to the Union patients, and there being no duly qualified man resident within the district, he be re-appointed medical officer of that district for a farther pericd of three years terminating on 19th September, 1910."

AN EASILY REDETERMINED STANDARD. Theoretically all our weights and measures, as well as those of the metric system, are re- ferable to natural standards, but the accu- rate measurement of and reference to these natural standards are no easy matters, and in practice artificial standards are used. If thezie Irtificial standards, however, were de- stroyed it would be very difficult to replace them, and it is interesting to note, therefore, tlfat the International Solar Union has adopted a natural standard of length which is not only unalterable, but which can at any time be made available for comparison pur- poses. This standard is the length of the waves which produce the light of red cad- mium the ten-thousand-millionth part of a metre !—and under the name of an " Ang- strom " it is now the unit used by solar physicists.

===== 0002601_19071116_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
POISONOUS PLANTS.

A large number of plants which are usually looked upon as harmless possess poisonous properties. Those who are employed in pick- ing daffodils and narcissus frequently suffer severely from a troublesome skin affection known as "daffodil poisoning." The most trying feature of this ailment, says a writer in the Grand Magazine, are the small gather- ings which form under the finger-nails. It is said that minute crystals of lime exist in , large numbers in the juice of the stems of the pretty blooms, and that these are responsible for the poisoning. Regular daffodil-pickers . are accustomed to rub their hands with oil ; and work 'tallow in underneath their finger- nails before beginning their work. The bulbs • of the white Roman hyacinth have similar unpleasant properties.

===== 0002601_19071116_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SOURCE OF STRONTIUM.

According. to a paper communicated to the Geological section of the British Association, a deposit in the Uppic Triassic of Abbots Leigh, near Bristol, has become the chief source of the world's supply of strontium. One of the distinguishing characters of this metal, and the property which has given it its commercial importance, is the intense crim- son colour which it imparts to a flame. It is hence largely used in pyrotechnics and for coloured flames used in signalling. At Abbots Leigh the strontia occurs in boulders of all sizes, from that of a pea up to 100 tons in weight. The estimated amount per acre is 2,000 tons.

===== 0002601_19071116_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
STRONTIUM 'ffi SUGAR MAKIN--

At one -Gime- stronlium seemed destined to play an important part in sugar making. In ordinary sugar refining the ultimate molasses, although it contains 30 to 40 per cent. of sugar, refuses to deposit this in the crystal- line form. Now according to Scheibler this sugar can be recovered by the addition of strontia. Hitherto, however, the process does not seem to have been adopted by sugar re- finers. Strontium, it may be noted, takes its name from Strontian, in Argyllshire, where one of its compounds, strontianite, was first discovered.

===== 0002601_19071116_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
ECCENTRICITIES OF SOUND.

'One would naturally suppose that a loud and continuous sound could be heard at about equal distances in all directions, in the ab- sence of wind, and if no large solid body in- terposed. Repeated experiments have shewn, says Science Siftings, that this is not true, and that mariners who trust to their ears alone when they are on dangerous coasts guarded by fog signals run a great peril. Sound shadows" are formed, within which the ear detects no trace of the signal, al- though the fog whistle may be blowing with full force, and the sound may be perfectly audible on all sides of the shadow. Some- times the cause of a sound shadow can readily be discovered, as, for instance, in the existence of a bluff just behind the whistle. In such a case the sound appears to ricochet like a cannon ball bounding over the water; in other words, it may be audible at one mile, inaudible at two miles, audible again at three miles, inaudible at four miles, and so on. But the intervals between the audible and _in- audible points may be any other distance, and may also vary irregularly. Then there are sound shadows the origin of which has not been traced, but which, doubtless, are due to some peculiar condition of the atmo- sphere.

===== 0002601_19071116_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
LIFE ON MARS.

A writer in the Scientific American says: For some unaccountable reason there seems to be a strong prejudice among both scien- tists and laymen against acknowledging the existence of a race of intelligent beings upon any planet other than our own. We cannot help thinking that our earth is the most favourably situated of the solar system, and is the best suited to support life. To be sure, this is so as regards life with which we are familiar; or, to state it more correctly, the animal and vegetable life of this earth has adjusted itself, its habits, and its require- ments into harmony with conditions already fixed upon earth. This is no argument that life cannot adjust itself to conditions such as are found on other planets. Those laymen who expected that the question of life on Mars would be settled by observations dur- ing the 1907 summer's favourable opposition, welt predestined to disappointment. No one who is familiar with the subject expected as much. It is highly improbable that we can ever prove with mathematical accuracy that animal life does exist upon the planet. It is far easier to prove the existence of vegetable life by the seasonal changes in the colour of large fields or forests. If these areas of vege- tation skew any unusual configuration and arrangement such :-is the " oases " and " canals " or "lanes of vegetation" on Mars, it- is not unreasonable to argue that the vege- tation is being cultivated or regulated by a race of intelligent beings. At the same time, the existence of such beings is not infallibly proved by such evidence. ,

===== 0002601_19071116_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PALOLO WORM.

Year by year, as the seasons pass on, says the Globe, there appear in the neighbourhood of the coral reefs of the South Pacific islands vast swarms of a curious green worm. Under the name of the Pablo worm, it has long been known to naturalists, though certain parts of its history have remained a mystery. So regular is its appearance that in Samoa it has given its name to the spring which is called vae-palolo," ,that is, the time of the palolo. And the coming of the palolo is eagerly looked forward to by the natives, for it is eaten and esteemed a great delicacy in those favoured places where it is wont to ap- pear. Its periodical appearance is apparently regulated by the moon, and the natives are able to predict the timo of its arrival by ob- serving the lunar changes. About three o'clock in the morning after the third quarter of the October moon, they appear in immense quantities. And after the third quarter of the November moon they appear again, though in smaller numbers.

CURIOUS LIFE HISTORY OF PALOLO.

When the palolo appears the natives put out to sea to gather the worms. For soon alter sunrise they disappear. Each female worm breaks up into a number of eggs. The males also break up, and the eggs are fer- tilised in the water. Nothing is left of the parent worms but some shrivelled fragments of skin. The young worms, being hatched, finally make their way into the interstices of the coral reefs, where they attain maturity. Such was supposed to be a complete account of their life history. Another remarkable point with regard to the palolo is that, though its coming is regulated by changes in the moon, yet in the long run it keeps solar time.

===== 0002601_19071116_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT A COMPLETE ORGANISM.

In the Bulletin of the Museum of Com- parative Zoology, Harvard, Mr. Woodworth gives the result of some investigations on the palolo. Mr. Woodworth's observations con- firm Ehler's results. The palolo is not a. complete organism. It is the detached gene- rative part of an annelid known as eunice viridis, a sort of sea-mouse. In the same communication it is stated that the day be- fore the appearance of the nalolo there ap- pears a large number ox a small headless worm. This has been tentatively named eunice dubia, and may be the reproductive parts of another species of sea-mouse , ip- habiting the coral reefs. _ . .

CHURCHMEN AND EDUCATION.

Under the auspices of the Warrington Church Society a lecture was delivered in St. Clement's Mission on Thursday evening by the Rev. J. B. Thorpe, 8.D., of Stockport, on "The Educational policy of the Church." There was a good attendance. The Rev. Canon Steven- son,who presided, said they were faced with the prospect of a new Education Bill next Session, but what that Bill might be they had not the means of knowing. In the meantime, he took it, they were all of one mind in the matter. They stood for justice and fair treatment all round. They stood for the liberty of the parent to have the child brought urrin what the parent believed to be right, and the liberty which they claimed for themselves they were (pally willing to yield to others. But for tbat liberty and that right they intended to stand together am' tight to the very end. (Applause.)—The Rev. J. H. Thorpe said they rejoiced to remember that the Muse of Commons was not the whole Government of this country. It was not the will of the Commons that had to govern England it was the will of the people. The House of Lords did their duty in amending the last Education Bill and whatever threats might be made against the Second Chamber, they should remember the good old proverb : "Threatened men live long." (Laughter.) Why should it now be an accredited part of the Liberal party's programme that when they were in power the Church of England was to-be attacked? A very large number of Churchmen were Liberals and he sincerely hoped they would continue to be. He thought it would be the greatest possible mistake if all the Churchmen were Tories. The time had gone by when Churchmen could put their trust in political parties of any kind. They should put their trust in God and in their own cause. It was a disgrace to the Churchmanship of Liberal Churchmen who did not protest to their party leaders against the principle that whenever the Liberal party was in power the Church of England was to be attacked. There would be no settlement of the education question so long as the Government approached it from an attitude of hostility to Churchmen. They could not accept any settlement which rested on the basis of a common Protestantism. His suggestion was that arrangements should be made to give facilities to meet four types of opinion on the question—Church of England, Roman Catholic, undenominationalists, and secularists. If facilities for any other kind of teaching were asked for they might be conceded if a sufficiently large number of parents desired them.—The lecturer was thanked on the pro- position of Mr. H. J. Westbrook, seconded by Mr. Firth, P.C.M.

CHESHIRE CHURCH SCHOOL Request to the County Committee.

The Cheshire Education Committee on Monday had before it an application by the Governing Body of Witton Grammar School, Northwich, under the regula- tions for secondary schools, suggesting that the Com- mittee should pass a resolution informing the Board of Education that Wittoh Grammar School was, in their view, required as part of the secondary school provision for that area, and that conditions 5 and 23 of the Regu- lations may be waived with advantage. The regulations referred to lay down that no catechism or formulary distinctive of any particular religious denomination may be taught in the school except in cases where the parent or guardian of any scholar requests it for the scholar, and also that the instrument under which the school Is governed must not require any members of the teaching staff to belong or not to belong to any particular denomination. The school was founded 350 years ago as a Church of England school. The Com- mittee by 25 votes to 13 decided to inform the Governors that it could not see its way to advise the Board of Education to grant the exceptional treatment asked for.

LATCHFORD WARD ELECTION. A Party Fight. The election in Latchford Ward to fill the vacancy created by the elevation of Councillor Ellison to an aldermanic seat will take place on Monday, November 25th. Five o'clock this (Saturday) afternoon is the latest hour for the receipt of nominations at the Town Clerk's office. Already the Liberals and Conserva- tives have candidates in the field and there is promise of a keen fight. Mr. William Bibby, of Arpley-street, a prominent Friendly Society worker, is the Liberal nominee and Mr. Henry Singleton, Bridge-street, will oppose him on behalf of the Conservatives. The only nomination papers handed in up to last evening were in support of Mr. Singleton who is proposed by John Coxon seconded by Fred W. Ward and assented to by Daniel Johnson, Alfred Grounds, James Smith Burgess, Alfred Chaplin, W. Hankey, Henry Arnold, Abraham Fenton and Albert Milne. In a second paper he is proposed by Councillor W. B. Roberts ani seconded by councillor W. J. Wilson. MR. BIBBY'S ADDRESS. In his address, which appears in our advertising columns, Mr. William Bibby points out that he lived in the ward twenty-five years. He promises to support the construction of a bridge at Howley and the exten- sion of Victoria Park. He takes the greatest interest in Friendly Societies and would do all in his power to defend and protect the same. He refers the electors to his services on the Education Committee and points out that as a Churchman he has the highest possible regard for the people who have built the schools and educated the children in the past, and he would not support any serious interference with the present method. He is not aware that the Warrington Education Committee has attempted to pr event any parent having his children educated in the same faith as himself, and if any attempt were made to do so he would oppose it. LIBERAL WARD MEETING. A meeting of the Latchford Liberal Ward Committee , was held in the Primitive Methodist Chapel last even- ing, Mr. NV. Roberts presiding.—Mr. William Bibby was asked to stand for the Ward, and consented to do ' so. He was proposed by Mr. F. Atkinson, and seconded by Alderman Luke Ellison, and on the motion being ; put to the meeting it was carried unanimously.—The 5 nomination papers on Mr. Bibby's behalf were signed.

Mr. Dodds writes :—" It is unnecessary, I think, for I ma to state that I should welcome the formation of a Temperance Electoral Council for Warrington, and that I should be prepared to further its objects by every legitimate means in my power. .-t,t ganchester and other places we see the leaders of " the Trade " calling upon its members and its servants to seek positions upon all public bodies in their own selfish interests. I have, for six years, steadily opposed members of our Town Council entering it to grind their own axes ; how much more am I called upon to combat the efforts of a traffic which is not only avowedly seeking its own ends, but which, in doing so, must of necessity, as all experience proves, demoralise and corrupt a large proportion of those of our citizens who are vaihappy enough to come' within the sphere of its blighting and pauperising influence."

November 14th, 1907. THE REV. JOHN YONGE. The Rev. J. Yonge writes.:—l quite agree with the paragraph in your last issue touching "The Trade and the Council," and am glad you inserted it. It is in my opinion a humiliation to find th,it the electors of at any rate two Wards in the Borough can be rallied so easily to the cause of "the Trade." And an especial humiliation to see that many of the said electors

imagin e they are standing by the supremely sacred causes of "Education and Religious Liberty " by returning two representatives of the Drink Traffic ! I earnestly hope that your suggestion of forming a "United Temperance Electoial Council" may find favour among thoughtful people of all sects and classes, and that such a Council may be created, and be able to influence our townspeople Were next November. What Newcastle-on-Tyne has done may be possible even for this Borough. This is not a question for total abstainers alone, but for all those who care for the sobriety of the town in which they live, and for the moral welfare of the people. Whatever may be in my power to do in the matter shall be willingly attempted. THE REV. J. F. MATTHEWS.

The Rev. J. F. Matthews writes : " I entirely approve of your leaderette of last week suggesting the formation of a local electoral Council, similar to that obtaining in Newcastle-on-Tyne. Indeed It is even more needed locally than in the Nortkern City. Orders have gone out that the 'Trade ' shculd seek representation on every public body. Why ? Simply in its own interests. It does not care a snap about public morals, or ethical ideals, or any of the great reforms so much needed in municipal life. It seeks to act and work through the Watch Committees and as far as possible the Magistracy too. Can snch committee be formed? That depends upon the strength of public spirit and the moral sentiment of the citizens. I shall greatly rejoice to se 3 its advent,2,nd would gladly help to bring it about." Baptist Church, Latchford. MR. E. J. GUEST, IP.

MR. b. R. GRIFFITHS%

Mr. Griffiths, of Moore, writes :—" In your columns -on Saturday you appeal for a Temperance Electoral Council. This is the crying need of Warrington, a need 'which was never more eiearly set forth than it is to-day. -ern writing in your columns early in June on "The -Churches and Tempehinee " I pointed out the same deed of such an organisation, and asked for the names of all those who would • i,ecome members. Had those interested Then come forward I honestly believe we -should not have been in theposition in which the results of theist 'net. have now plaaad us. Some will assert that there ii not seificient " temperance sentiment" in the town. Here, however, our .eritics labour under a delusion. Warrington has in bygone yeers been the home of the pioneers of the total abstainers and Temperance movement. To-day their influence is with us. There is suffielent•temperance sentiment in the town, which if set to work could swamp," the Trade" every time the Brewer candidates come forward. In fact have been surprised to find the number of people in every grade. who hold dear the principles of the Temperance Party. But well may our critics ask: "Why do reeeilts not prove it?" For the simple reason that the Temperance people of Warrington are divided into so many sections. It may lee urged that the results in Howley and Town Hail go to prove that the trend of public opinion is in the other direction. This is not the case for had it not been for the "religious red herring," better housing and other influences drawn across the path of electors at tbe last moment. the defeni, of -" the Trade" in Town Hail Ward was assured and Howley would have had a very narrow margin. I read the account of the contests at Newcastle, and I must certainly thank you for placing it before your readers. Well may we ask will Warrington Electors take the hint? I say they can do it and the day is coming when they 'mil/ do it. They are waiting for the Temperance party to take the lead. Your call has come at a time when it was never more deeply felt. It is quite clear from the most recent platform and pulpit ' utterances that we have every reason for hope—at hope which must not be crushed if we ever intend to have a sober and a righteous town, I hope that as a result of the call some definite step will be taken to form an organisation which will be a credit to the town which played a Tioble paTt at the birth of this world.wido movement,

LOCAL CHURCHES. Services for Sunday.

Bold-street Wesleyan Church : Morning'lo.3o, Mr. Champness ; evening 6.30, Rev. Thomas Miller. Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church: Morning 10.30, Rev. John Gunnell ; evening 6.30, Mr. J. Champness. Wycliffe Church, Bewsey-street (foreign missionary anniversary) : Morning 10.30, Rev. J. Yonge; evening 6.30, Rev. E. Pryce-Jones. In the afternoon Mr. Jones will deliver an address to the scholars and teachers.

Baptist Church, Latchford: Preacher, morning 10.30, evening 6.0, Rev. J. F. Mathews. Morning subject, "The sign manual of Human Redemption." Evening subject, Translated-: An in memoriam service." Baptist Church, Gelborne-street : Morning 10.45, evening 6.30, preacher, Rev. R. E. Lotheren. _

Rev. J. F. Matthews and Municipal Matters. At the Latchford Baptist Church, on Sunday evening, the Rev. J. F. Matthews said they had all seen with pleasure that their friend, Mr. Luke Ellison, had been raised by the Town Council to that vacant aldermanic chair. They were all glad that so good a man had at last been thus honoured. That occasion was the earliest opportunity that had occurred of referring to the recent municipal elections in the borough. He was amazed at the return of two men to the Council who were directly connected with the drink traffic, and still more so that an alderman had again been elected on the Watch Com- mittee. He thought it a disgrace that public sentiment had so little strength, that as a mere question of good taste, any man should allow himself to be elected to a Watch Committee, when he was so intimately associated with the trade. He earnestiy asked every member of that congregation who had a vote, not to use it at any time, for any person directly or indirectly associated with the drink traffic.

Confirmation Services at Warrington.

The Lord Bishop of Liverpool (Dr. Cnavase) visited Warrixgton on Tuesday and conducted two confirmation services. In the afternoon at Holy Trinity Church, 61 candidates were presented from Holy Trinity Church, four from Wargrave Church, and one from Glazebury Church. The clergy present were: were the Revs. E. C. E. Carleton (vicar), H. R. Wild and B. Morgan Price (curates), G. Feather, of Glazebury, and W. Carson, of Wargrave. The Bishop held a confirmation service in the evening at St. Barnabas' .Church where the number of candidates was as follows: St. Barnabas', 25 ; St. Paul's, 19; St. Peter's 1 ; and St. Ann's, 5. There was a good congregation. The clergy present were the Revs. .T. If. G. Hollingworth (vicar), W. Bracecamp and E. E. Peters (St. Paul's) and H. Russell (St. Peter's). The Bishop delivered very impressive addresses in which he urged the confirmees to cleave to Christ, to the Bible, and to tiva Church of England.

FREE CHURCHES AND FLUNKEYISM.

Rev. J. F. Matthews' Spirited Comments

During the first of a series of addresses on the Epistle of James delivered ia Golborne-street Baptist Church, on Wednesday evening, the Rev. J. F. Matthews said that perhaps there was to-day no form of tow-towing, no form of toadying at once so despicable and sickening, as the way in which many Christians " real and alleged" fawned upon the rich. If it were the strong, beautiful character of the rich man to which they paid reverence it could be easily understood. The most ludicrous sight was the hasty and almost abject way in which church officers hastened to pay court to the well-to-do who came into their church- buildings. No seat was too cosy for them. No atten- tion was too much to offer them. If any distinction had to be made between the pharisee and the publican let the publican be the honoured man. If they wanted to offer a good seat to anybody let the poor man have the luxury. He (the lecturer) held fast to the belief, that when people entered the four walls of a church they should be treated as they were in the Master's Kingdom. No man should be specially honoured because of his wealth or social position. The rank is but the guinea stamp

The man's the gowd for a'that - The Free Churches seemed to be the hot bed for this peculiar form of flunkeyism. As soon as they got hold of a rich man they put him in office. The actual ques- tion of character was often a secondary matter; the world sneered, and ridiculed that attitude. Let it be made clear in days to come that the rich man was not honoured because of his banking account, or the large donations he gave, but because of his native worth. To sum up, said the lecturer, a character of rare beauty, niade patient by long endurance of suffering and per- secution ; a wisdom won by a loving waiting upon the infinite Source and Giver of all wisdom ; the relative places of the rich and the poor, and the equitable treat- ment of them, were the topics that came up at once for consideration in the first part of chapter I. Let them embody the truths they learnt there, in the daily routine of life.

On behalf —;4 Sir Gilbert (Treenail, Bart., a sale was held by Mr. W. 134.inbridge of pedigree large and middlebreed boars, sows, and gilts at Walton Hall, on Thursday. The principal buyers were the Earl of Sefton, Mr. Frascr (Huyton), Mr. T. Ball (Neston), and Mr. J. Newton (Warrington). Many of the animals were prize winners. Joseph Crosfield and Sons Limited. For interests, with what object we leave the perspicacious the benefit of those who contend that manufacturers I reader to conclude for hiinself. would be " hampered "if preventive measures were forced upon them, we cannot do better than quote from an article on " Smoke prevention and fuel economy " which Dr. K. E. Markel, one of the managing directors of the firm, contributed to a recent number of "Progress." He wrote : —With regard to factory smoke—which is responsible for the balk of the nuis- ance—it is more difficult to generalize in view of the variety and complexity of conditions, but it is still true that, with very few exceptions, there is a cure for every smoky chimney, and that efforts to abate smoke are accompanied by an economy which, in many cases, dwarfs into insignificance the initial expenditure entailed. A striking instance is that of my own firm, Joseph Crosfield and Sons, of Warrington. We are using at the present moment over a , thousand tons less coal per week than would be the case under former conditions, a saving, when the cost of handling and burning is taken into account, of about £25,000 per annum—and all this as a corollary to smoke prevention. Years ago we • were constantly being fined for creatinst a nuisance: to-day our works are known as "The Works with the Smoke. less Chimneys." Obviously such a result —for our case was no worse than that of hundreds of other old- established industries—could only be obtained by employing all the resources of science, it goes with- out saying that we have introduced modern appliances and eliminated as far as possible the human equation by automatic devices, but the main secret of our success is that, grasping boldly the principle that combustion is a chemical re-action, we have placed the whole plant under a competent well- paid chemical staff. The fuel is under scientific observation from its entry into the works till it escapes from the chimney, or is removed as ash. The coal is carefully sampled and tested for calorific value, percentage of moisture, and amount of ash, our contracts are made on the basis of these tests, and future deliveries must be to standard. The Hue gases are- by special contrivances frequently and regularly sampled at various points, and also analysed. So it is with our feed-water, which is regularly tested, treated accordingly, and filtered. The resuft is that the boilers never scale ; twice a year only they are stopped, when all that is necessary is to brush them out, and some that have been w a•kina nearly 20 years are as clean and practically as good as when new. To water purification I attach the highest importance, for it is absurd to expect a boiler to

respond to demands on it when it is thickly scaled with non-conducting substance ; further it may be taken that in many works one boiler out of every five or six is always in the hands of the cleaner, and a very large amount of smoke is caused by the firemen pushing insufficient or inefficient boilers to get out of them more steam than they can reasonab!y be expecte,' to generate. The chief reforms Dr. Mirkel would insist cn are : 1. The substitution wherever possible of automatic stoking for hand firing. 2. Employment of intelligent trained stokers. They should be well paid, and might be encouraged by bonuses on results scientifically tested. 3. Provision of courses for firemen, as in Prussia, with practical lessons concurrently. 4. Manufacturers might with great advantage place their plants under a competent chemist-engineer, or seek the advice of one. This should certainly be done in the case of all large plants. • 5. The law should be strengthened. Smoke may be less than black yet an intolerable nuisance. The wording, again, admits too great a latitude of local interpretation : 15 minutes of blaek 8 moke in the hour as allowed at Middlesbrough is a clear evasion of the spirit of the Act. Further, the exemptions are held by some manufacturers to excuse any smoke whether necessary or not, so that in some cases the Act is a dead letter. It is, lam aware, a costly and difficult thing to modernise the methods of our

LOCAL MUNICIPAL QUESTIONS. COUNCILLOR HAWTHORN'S INTERESTING REVIEW Warrington Bridge and other _ Questions _Discussed.

At the meeting of the Warrington Junior Liberal Association on Mondayevening, Councillor J. Hawthorn gave a highly interesting address on " Local Municipal Questions." Mr. J. Darbyshire presided over a good attendance.

slums; it was the governing bodies who were respon- 1 sible. In Warrington the byelaws had been broken time after time. It was owing to the lack of rigid application of the laws that a great many slums had been created. Property owners had taken advantage of it and there had been neglect all round. He was not attacking the present Corporation. Those who ruled us twenty or thirty years ago were most to blame, but the present authorities were to blame for unpaned streets and dilapidated property. The rateplyers ought also to insist that the Corporation did its duty. They had been too apathetic on the housing question. Mr. GUEST expressed regret that the recent municipal elections were largely fought on a question which did not come within the scope of a Councillor's work. It was a disgrace that people should be elected to the Council simply because they disapproved of an Act which had not yet been passed. Votes of thanks were accorded to Mr. Hawthorn and to the Chairman.

Owing to the debate on the peace question and the old-age pension demonstration, there will be no meeting of the Association on Monday evening next.

Councillor HAWTHORN dealt with his subject very ably. He said the task before the members of the Corporation was an onerous and burdensome one— burdensome to some extend owing to legacies of the past. The narrow and winding thoroughfares though which the stage coach rumbled had to he widened and straightened, old buildings and condemned sanitary arrangements removed and perfected. In this work the Council needed to be on their guard against committing some of the errors of their forefathers in not anticipat- ing the needs of a future generation. With the results of the recent municipal elections still fresh iu their minds, and the cries of great Tory victories, many might be disposed to ask : What have party politics to do with such matters as street making sewering, or controlling oar municipal undertakings? Alluding , proficient at lawn tennis, of which she is very fond ; to some of the expensive improvements which lie , ahead of the Corporation, Councillor Hawthorn said indeed it was on the Tennis Courts at St. Moritz that : ' As yet we have only completed a portion of our street Miss Elliadi and Mr. Crosfield first met. wideoinescheme. Next year, I hope, will see the coma While in L mdan Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield will live pletion of the Bridge-street scheme and we have already t 39, Hyde Park Gate. made a start in Sankey-street. Two of our main streets a are, however, still left untouohed and these will require attention at the earliest opportunity. Then we shall have to face the housing problem more seriously and determinedly if we intend to reduce the death rate, for I believe the proper housing of our people lies at the root of our difficulties in this respect. The tramways have helped the middle Classes to get into the suburbs, but there is still overcrowding in the oldest parts of the town and many of the dwellings are damp and deficient in their sanitary arrangements. There is also the still larger question of the disposal of refuse and I drainage looming in the future. do not profess to have any prophetic vision, but I think I may without presuming too much say that the chief, topics likely to receive attention during the year are : (1.) The widening or re-construction of Warrington Bridge. (2) The provision of a public abattoir. (3) A re-arrangement of some of our municipal offices, including the utilisation of Garven House in Sankey-street. (4) The joint scheme of a school add recreation ground in Lovely-lane. (5) Small Holdings and Allotments. I have placed the subject of Warrington Bridge in the foremost place because of the urgency of this public improvement. There have been discussions innumerable in the Town Council; sub-committees and deputations have been frequently appointed; plans have been pre- pared and professional services engaged, and yet despite all this initial expense the question of doing something practical to relieve the present situation makes vary slow progress. There has been no lack of ideas, and like the dog in the fable we seem likely to miss the substance of one bridge through gazing at the shadows of two or three bridges. Before anything practical is done at Bridge Foot we shall have to dismiss from our minds all talk of new bridges at Howley or Arpley and focus atten- tion on Warrington Bridgo. To widen tho existing bridge by the erection of cantilevers would relieve the pressure, but this scheme can only be regarded as a temporary expedient. The scheme may serve its pur- pose for a number of years and then be discarded for a new concrete bridge of ampler proportions gracefully spanning the river with a single arch. We are now waiting patiently to hear the decision of the acting Conservator of the Mersey on the modified scheme of cantilevers added to the existing bridge, but if his decision is unfavourable the public will have good reason to press the Corporation to face the !question of constructing an entirely new bridge, in the interests of public safety and general convenience. Referring to the abattoir question, Councillor Hawthorn continued, the public has endured the evils of the old system of private slaughter-houses long enough. People will no longer be content to consume meat which has not been subjected to adequate inspeq- tion and will not run the risk of injury to health through want of a system of proper supervision. Most up-to-date towns are now equipped with a public abattoir or public slaughter-house where meat inspection is compulsory, and the tendency is for questionable cattle which might not pass the eye of an inspector in a public abattoir to be sent to towns where private slaughter-houses exist, and where the inspection is con- sequently less rigid., because it is impossible for inspectors to keep a vigilant eye on a score of private slaughter-houses at the same time. No municipality which professes to keep abreast of the times can dis- regard the movement for the establishment of an abattoir where the management will be above suspicion and proper supervision exercised. Councillor Hawthorn commended the proposals for the centralization of the Corporation offices and the provision of a new school and recreation gronnd in Lovely-lane. Continuing he said the formation of a small holdings and allotments committee marked a new era in the history of the Corporation and our experience in working the Small Holdings Act, passed by the Government in the last session of Parlia- ment,would help us to realise the difficulties of the land problem in this country even if it did not materially assist in a solution. The Town Clerk was shortly to report on the working of the Act which comes into force on the first \ January next, and there was eery reason to believe that the Town Council would make good use of the powers conferred upon them. It was a matter for congratulation to young Liberals that the Government was endeavouring to bring the landless man and manless land into closer affinity. The Corporation concluded,Councillor Hewthorn,may accomplish all this and much more during the year upon which we have just entered. The paving of our thoroughfares is dot all that could be desired, but we must not forget that the heavy traffic passing over the Warrington streets makes it impossible for the Cor- poration to attain to the high standard obtained by the County Council. The black smoke nuisance, too, has not had the consideration it deserves, but there are signs that this disagreeable feature of our commercial life will receive stricter attention. I know there are many who are disposed to criticise the slow progress made by the Corporation, but despite the slow move- ment of the Corporate machine, progress is being made, and I believe in the right direction. As one who was born in Warrington over 30 years ago I can see signs of progress on every hand and I do not think our municipal affairs were ever better managed than they are to-day. (Applause.) THE DISCUSSION. AN ELECTIVE AUDITOR'S CRITICISMS. An interesting discussion followed, in which the Chairman, Messrs. J: Adams, E. England, H. Ford, R. E. I3urtt, IT. W. Guest, C. Morrell, and A. Albinson took part. There was a general agreement with the views advanced by Mr. Hawthorn. Mr. ENGLAND said that after studying the history of Warrington he had no hesitation in saying that the town had not been enterprising by any means. We had suffered from over-cautiousness. He believed that if over twenty years ago the scheme for the deepening of the river as far as Warrington had been carried out, we would probably not have had the Manchester Ship Canal and Warrington would have been an important port. That scheme would not have cost Warrington half a million of money but the benefits to the town and district would have been estimated in millions. When such questions as the Warrington Bridge Improvement were brought forward the bogey of the rates was always raised. He believed the town was prepared to go in for a new bridge. Money had been wasted on making inquir,ol. There had been unnecessary deputations, and plan after plan had been drawn up—in fact over £l,OOO had been spent on the idea of having a bridge. Mr. England strongly advocated the establishment of an abattoir and cattle market, and contended that if the schemes were worked in conjunction there would be a saving to the town. With regard to the slum question, he honestly thought too much fault had been attributed to the tenants. It was a delusion and a snare to say that drink was the sole cause of slums. He admitted that it helped to make a slum district worse, but sociology and experience taught that as a rule people who were born in the slums remained slum-dwelleri. it was not the tenant pr the property owners who generally made

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. " Seconlii. None."

Mr. Walter Ho'ard's latest drama "Second to None" is a stirring story of military life of absorbing interest, and its presentation at the Royal Court Theatre this week by Mr. T. Edward Ward's company has given great pleasure to large audiences. Nell Greystone, daughter of Sir Reginald Greystone, V.C., Colonel ()tithe Royal Scots Greys, is wooed by Harry Wilding, a dashing young fellow who has made up his mind to gain glory as a soldier, and returns his love. Harry is sent to Sandhurst, but it is announced that he has failed in the examination for an officer's commis- sion, while his brother Frank passes with flying colours. Nothing daunted, however, Harry determines to enter the army by the "back door" and work his way up from the ranks, and his sweetheart promises to wait until he has attained his ambition. All is going well until the intervention of Captain Philip Mannering, whose suit has\ been rejected by Nell Greystone, and who speedily I assumes the villain's part. By eaves-dropping he , becomes acquainted with a black page in the Colonel's life and thus obtains a power over him which he exercises unmercifully. Young Harry Wilding is turned adrift, leads a wretched life in the army through Mannering's brutality and the Colonel's helplessness, and at last, in order to save her father from disgrace, Nell reluctantly promises to marry Mannering. 'Thais is not to be be, however, and after many exciting inci- dents villainy is thwarted and the drama ends with the marriage of Harry and Nell, while to crown all it transpires that Harry had passed the examination but some blunder on the part of the authorities deprived him of his just reward. The play is presents' by an excellent company. Miss Ward, an actress of repute, gives a highly acceptable portrayal of Nell Greystone, infusing considerable emotion into the many touching passages. Mr. Jerrold Heather as Harry Wilding, is an ideal lover and soldier and well deserves the warm applause which has nightly rewarded his efforts. All the other parts are capably sustained, Mr. Alfred Brandon making the most of the unpopular char- acter of Captain Mannering. Mr. J. G. Maine presents a very clever and wierd study of Sergeant Jack Horseman. The play is not without its humorous touches and the perpetual warfare between Joe Ruggles, the footman who is always "having a bit on " the All Blacks, and Private Flutter, his rival for the hand of Norah, the housemaid, creates midi merriment.

-NEXT 'WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Mr. W. W.—Kelly's " A Royal Divorce" Company, from the Scala Theatre; London, including Miss Beth Elliott in her wonderful impersonation of [impress Josephine. and Mr. Fred Victor as Napleon, will appear next week. Mr. Kelly has spared no expense in mount- ing the play in the most magnificent manner possible. The scenery is entirely new, from the study of Mr. W. T. Elemsley, and two magnificent tableaux of the Battle of Waterloo have been painted by Mr. Charles Pell. The dresses worn by Miss Beth Elliott as the Empress Josephine are from the famous house of Paquin, Paris. The other costumes and uniforms by Messrs. Morris, Angel and Son are remark- ably handsome, while in every detail the ensemble is exceptionally beautiful and has 'cost over £2,000. Originally produced I.G years ago, " A Royal Divorce" was an immediate success at the Olympic Theatre, London. After a long run it was transferred to the Princess' Theatre, and subsequently under Mr. Kelly's direction the play toured the world with the record of cawing the biggest crowds known in theatre history.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. RUGBY FOOTBALL. By -`o.valist."

Warrington are gradually improving their position in the League table, and consistency should certainly land them in one of the four leading places ere long. They are sixth at present, and ought to have little difficulty in displacing Barrow and Batley, the two clubs miniediately above them. To-day's match with Rochdale Hornets should bring a couple more points, for they have already beaten the Hornets on their own ground.

The visit to South Wales was productive of victory. Ebbw Vale played a vigorous game and for a long time kept Warrington at bay, but in the end class told its own tale and the wire-pullers won comfortably by 16 points to nil. The cup-holders had a fine reception in the valley, a crowd of 6,000 people witnessing a splendid exhibition of the N.U. game. Several old friends were found including Llandaff Smith and Jack Davies. Warrington laid themselves out to give an attractive display, and they succeeded. Rarely have they played better combined football, and the locals were quite content to be defeated by so superior a team. At half-time Warrington only led by a solitary goal placed by Thomas, but four tries came afterwards, the scorers being Brooks (2), Taylor and Boardman. Kenyon made a splendid run through the Ebbw Vale team and over the dead ball line, which wae'fi piece of misfortune.

All the Warrington men played well, bat the best of the lot was Brooks whose play transcended everything else seen in the match. He sprang right into the form which earned him his selection for the County. Jenkins supported ,him well and I am inclined to think that the Welshman would make a better partner than Sammy Lees. Cartwright took Isherwood's place in the centre and played a capital game, while Taylor adapted himself cleverly to the wing position. Ebbw Vale's best man was Higgins, the new half-back from Pill Harriers, who showed distinct promise.

The All Blacks sustained their first defeat on Saturday, and Wigan are entitled to the full credit for proving that the tourists are not invincible even to a good club team. It was a great game watched by about the biggest crowd ever seen at a Northern Union match The Central Park ground was a sight not to be readily forgotten when 30,000 people surrounded the arena. The receipts, £1,500, of which the New Zealanders take over :c,1,000, should com- pensate for the bitterness of the defeat.

I must say,, the All Blacks took the reverse like sportsmen, and goodness knows they had a terrible gruelling, for the Wigan men tackled with the ferocity of tigers. It was their tackling more than anything else which contributed to the downfall of the All Blacks. The latter were beaten for possession by the lighter Wigan forwards, and the backs were fairly smothered and put off their game. Several of them— notably Messenger and Rowe—seemed to be off-form in addition and the consequence was that the cam- binfition went t.) pieces. I did not think there was so much between the teams as some critics have made out. Wigan certainly had all the luck both in scoring and in preventing their opponents from scoring, and if the All Blacks had got a try just after the interval, as they very nearly did, there might have be9n a different tale to tell. They certainly finished full of fight, and scored the last points of the game almost on time. Leytham had a great day, and scored his three tries in clever fashion, but he had a very weak man to face in Messenger, and one of the points at least should have been neutralised for a forward transfer. However, it's all in the game, and the referee missed several little things of this description by which the All Blacks suffered. They were certainly not favoured on Saturday.

One thing struck me forcibly in the match at Wigan, and that was that the five-eighths game was a failure behind a beaten pack. It may have advantages in developing au attack when the half-back is getting the ball regularly, but on Saturday I thought the forma- tion had a lot to do with the ineffectiveness of the All Blacks' combination. One half-back was only able to do half as much spoiling as two would have done, and the consequence was that the Wigan halves had

rather a gay time. On the other hand when the New Zealand half did get possession, he rarely got away with the ball, so well did the home backs spot their men. The New Zealand insides would certainly have got the ball quicker if they had had two men at the base of the scrum, ae.cl thus stood a better chance of breaking through. smith was rJpeatedly in difficulties for this very reason. He received the ball when every avenue of escape was closed and as a result we saw several speedy runs across the field which were good .to look at but ineffective as an attacking measure. The prime causes of the All Blacks' failure were however, the inability of the forwards to heel out promptly, and the persistent attempts at picking up in the loose when footwork was certainly demanded as a tactical measure. A good dribble by speedy forwards is yet to be pre- ferred to indiscriminate passing. The lessons of Saturday should do our doughty foes good. Meanwhile they might as well look forward to another stiff hurdle when they meet the cup-holders at Warrington. Sol-1:t1. West Lancashire is as hot a district as they will visit on this tour.

Warrington's team to-day against Rochdale Horne ts will be: Tilley, full back; Taylor, Dickenson, Isherwood and Kenyon, three-quarters; Lees and Brooks, half backs; Thomas, Shugars, Boardman, 13- Boardman, Belton and Harmer, forwards.

Fish's suspension has expired, but as he has made no real effort toget himself into fit playing condition, the Committee are determined not to put him on the team.

A. strong "A." team has been chosen to do duty ill the Combination match at Rochdale,and I fully expect them to win. The selection is: Jordan, full back ; Harrison, Landor, Cartwright, and Tinkler, three- quarters ; Jenkins and Daintith, half backs; Foster, Jolley, Hewitt, Bradshaw, Holbrook and Morley, forwards.

A second defeat at Barrow on Thursday suggests that the All Blacks have struck a bad patch. Though Barrow have not lost on their own ground this season, they are not a team with any -pretensions to the highest class and the New Zealanders should have been equal to a win at Cavendish Park This second failure suggests that there is a screw loose somewhere. The visitors are certainly not showing the form which distinguished them in their first half dozen matches. Stricter training and less feasting and junketing would probably have a beneficial effect. The All Blacks have another severe engagement to-day at Hull, following which they go to Leigh on Saturday next. The hope is general that. they will not lose many more matches before coming to War- rington, but as they have ten engagements to fulfil before December 21st it will require phenomenal form to maintain the New Zealand reputation.

Another young Warrington player, E. Legge, haE migrated to Wigan, but rumour says that all is n9t gold that glitters, even at Central Park, and there is friction already.

Spectators will doubtless be interested in seeing Brooks and Baxter, the County half backs, ju opposition to-day at Warrington. Hockey.

The Stockton Heath first team journeyed to Crewe to meet the Permanent Way team on Saturday last. The Crewe ground was in a very bad condition, the ground on both wings being under water. Of course, this seriously interfered with the enjoyment of the game, and after a hard struggle the respective teams retired after scoring two goals each. Richardson scored both goals for the visitors.

-- The second team of the Club entertained Crewe second Wain at the cricket ground. The homesters scored theit first goal in the first five minutes of the game, and immediately afterwards Crewe also scored. The plaY after this point was very even, but the visitors eventually got through the Stockton defence and scored another goal. Result, Crewe 2 goals, Stockton Heath goal.

PENKETH.

ACCIDENT TO A BOY.—Leonard Fisher, aged 9. of Cuerdley.-road, Penketh, had a narrow escape from serious injury yesterday. He was swinging behind & cart in Station-road when his foot was caught in the wheel and badly bruised. His boot was torn off. _

..----- _ WESLEYA.N CHAPEL.—The Penketh Men's Bible Clans hold their open service on Sunday in the above chapel and were honoured with a visit from Mr. Watson. F.R.A.S., of Warrington, who treated the congregation to a most interesting address, his subject being The sanctity of human labour." The address was edifying' throughout. Mr. James Dodds took the chair. HIGHWAY ASSAULT. —On Saturday night at about ten o'clock a party of boys and girls, consisting of John and Dolly Baker (son and daughter of Mr. J. W. Baker, butcher, Penketh), Ethel Midwood and Walter Foden, were returning home from a birthday party when theY. were attacked by two men between Doe Green and Whitfleld's Farm. These men who were walking towards Widnes shouted to them to stop and one of the party, John Baker, who was riding a bicycle, was suddenly knocked over the handle-bars, receiving injurl to his knee and the machine being damaged. His sister, Dolly Baker, screamed. Eventually both the assailants made off across the fields in the direction of Widnes. The party hastened home much frightened and the outrage was reported to the police. MISSIONARY MEETING.—On Thursday evening, under the auspices of the Wesleyan Methodist Missions*. Society, a lecture with cinematograph views was held in the Wesleyan Chapel, representing Missionary work and life in China, with living pictures. Mr. R. Posnett of Runcorn, occupied tbe chair. The Rev. John Gunnell read the report, which was highly sntisfactorY, Pefiketh's total amount contributed to Foreign Mission being £64 10s. 7d., an increase over last year of £l2 2s. 9d. The Rev. E. J. Gawne officiated as lecturer, Mr. Gardner being the operator of the lantern. Thel was a very good audience, and a most enjoyable instructive evening was brought to a close about 9.;." when Mr. J. G. Withinsbaw moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Posnett for presiding, and Mr. C. E. Parker secondod.—The collections, which were in aid of foreign missions, amounted to over £l3 Bs.

`COACULINE," ELINK,"TEXASITINE.'--Cements for broken and other articles.—Advt. Taxation of Land Values. The second meeting of the season of the Warrington Bratich of the English League for the Taxation of Land Values was held on Wednesday night. As it had been decided at a previous committee meeting that the subject of the taxation of land values should be treated on the lines laid down by Henry George in his boot: Progress and Poverty. Mr. W. H. Sherburri, President of the Warrington Branch opened out a discussion on the problem of poverty and wealth. A. very enthusinstic discusskn followed in which it was agreed that though the labour saving inventions of the present day allowed the labourer to produce many times more than he could under former conditionb,still his struggl° for a decent living was if anything Larder than it had, ever been. The question of why the advantages 0' labour saving machinery do not acme to the labourer as they naturally ought is to be the subject of discussion at the next monthly meeting.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. N I CRIf; RAoLTDBRsY 1,1;14:LLI i&CTICIH7O RD I"W7 A—RTDO THF, LADIES AND GENTI,MEN, I have been requested by many Friends and also by the Liberal Ward Committee to become a candidate to fi'l the vacancy caused by the elevation of my Friend, Mr. Luke Ellison to the Aldermanic Bench. and hive consented to do so. I come from a very old Warrington family and have lived in the Ward for 25 years. I am a small Property Owner in the Ward and have taken taken the greatest interest as Secretary and President of the Liberal Ward Committee for over 20 years. I know the many rerprirernents of the Ward—Victoria Bridge, the Extension of Victoria Park, the New Bridge at Howley, and many others. These improvements I consider of the first importance,and would do my best to bring them about. 1 take the greatest interest in Friendly Societies, and would do all fn my power to defend and protect same. have served for three years on the reducation Committee as the first representative of the United Friendly Societies of the town, being chosen by a Meeting of Representatives of all the Societies at the Town Hall. I was a member of the School Attendance and Finance Sub-Committees, and my attendance during the three years was near the highest of 36 members forming the Council, and am at present a manager for two Schools in the Ward—St. James' and Grappenhall-road Council Schools. As a Churchman I have the highest possible regard for the people who have built the Schools and educated our Children in the past, and I would not support any serious interference with the present method. lam not aware that the Warrington Education Committee have attempted to prevent any Parent having his Children educated in the same faith as himself, and if any attempt was made to do so I should oppose it. consider the work that has been done by the Education Committee is splendid, and has been accomplished with little or no friction, which is a great thing when you come to consider that many shades of Religion and Politics are represented. With regard to other Corporation undertakings, I sheuld support what I consider to be in the best interests of the town generally. If elected I shall do my best to serve you and my native town faithfully and well. Soliciting the favour of your votes and support, I beg to remain. Ladies and Gentlemen, Yours respectfully, WILLIAM BIBBY, 13, Arpley Street, Warrington.
